Job-switch handbook

From zero to the interview

Sahil Siddiqui
https://www.linkedin.com/in/sahil-siddiqui-8416b8195/

This book teaches arrays and matrices in JavaScript. Each chapter uses only ideas from earlier chapters. You start by indexing a list. You finish by filling a grid in the order the cells depend on.

One learning path  ·  Stay, then Then, then a second pass

  1. 00 The arrayYou become able to index, sort, and tell a subarray from a subsequence.
  2. 01 Read the problemYou become able to classify a prompt in four lines before any code.
  3. 02 One passYou become able to answer each index from a running summary.
  4. 03 Two loopsYou become able to enumerate every pair and every subarray.
  5. 04 Hash mapYou become able to replace an inner search with a lookup.
  6. 05 Pointers from the endsYou become able to discard one end of a sorted range.
  7. 06 Same-direction pointersYou become able to overwrite an array with a read index and a write index.
  8. 07 Sliding windowYou become able to grow and shrink one contiguous band.
  9. 08 Prefix sumsYou become able to answer a range sum, including when negatives appear.
  10. 09 Difference arrayYou become able to add a value on many ranges, then read the array once.
  11. 10 A left pass and a right passYou become able to combine two directional summaries.
  12. 11 KadaneYou become able to take the best contiguous sum, even if every value is negative.
  13. 12 Sort, then scanYou become able to pay n log n so pairs and intervals have an order.
  14. 13 Place each valueYou become able to put a value at the index where it belongs.
  15. 14 Binary searchYou become able to search an index, or search the answer itself.
  16. 15 Monotonic stackYou become able to find the next greater element in one pass.
  17. 16 Greedy scansYou become able to track a reach, a jump layer, or a tank.
  18. 17 RecognitionYou become able to name the chapter from the wording.
  19. 18 The matrixYou become able to index rows, columns, diagonals, and four directions.
  20. 19 Walk the matrixYou become able to spiral, transpose, and rotate.
  21. 20 In-place marksYou become able to store the next state inside the cell.
  22. 21 2D prefixYou become able to sum any rectangle, then collapse a matrix to a 1D map.
  23. 22 Sorted matrixYou become able to binary-search a flat index, or walk a staircase.
  24. 23 RegionsYou become able to flood a region and run a multi-source search.
  25. 24 Filling orderYou become able to fill a cell only after the cells it depends on.
  26. 25 Four weeksYou become able to order the practice and sit a mixed mock.

Before chapter 0

How to study this book

Read one chapter at a time. Close the page and rewrite the template before you solve that chapter's problems.

How a chapter is built

Each pattern chapter has nine parts. They always come in the same order, so you know where to look.

The first part says what the pattern is. It states the idea in plain words, before any code. The second part says what you already know. It names the earlier chapter this one uses. If you cannot rewrite that earlier template from a blank file, do that rewrite before you start.

The third part is a worked example, and it comes before the code. This is the part to read slowly. The example takes a small input and walks it one step at a time. You see which position you are on, what you update, and what is stored when the step ends. The code later is a compact form of this walk. If you skip the walk, you will not see why one line has to run before the next.

The fourth part is how to recognise the pattern. It lists the signs in a prompt that point to this chapter, and it names the lookalike that belongs in another chapter. The fifth part is headed What to say out loud. Those are two to four sentences you can say in an interview before you write code.

The sixth part is the JavaScript template. The lines with it say what the important lines do. Read those lines beside the example. The seventh part is time and extra memory. Time is how the number of steps grows as the list gets longer. Extra memory is space you use besides the input. A few variables are a small cost. A second copy of the whole list is a different cost, and the chapter says so when that happens.

The eighth part is mistakes. These are the errors this pattern invites. After you rewrite the template, check your code against that list. The ninth part is a practice ladder. It is the problem list, already in order. Start at the top.

Some chapters contain more than one template. Each template still has an example, the code, and a cost. You read each block the same way. The worked example is still the part to read slowly.

The three lanes

Every practice problem sits in one lane. The three lanes are Stay, Then, and Second pass. The lane is printed on the problem. It tells you when that problem is your work.

A Stay problem drills the template you just rewrote. Stay is required. A Stay problem is never premium-only. Premium means LeetCode locks the problem statement behind a paid account, so a free account cannot open it. This book does not put a locked problem in Stay.

A Then problem is the same chapter with one extra demand. You might handle a carry, a count, or a wrap from the last position back to the first. You do Then after Stay. Then is still first-pass work.

A Second pass problem is a harder use of the same idea. Second pass waits until the path is finished. The path is chapter 0 through chapter 25. Leave these problems until that path is done.

First pass means Stay plus Then. You finish both before you open the next chapter. Second pass is a later round. It starts the week after you finish chapter 24. Chapter 25 is the calendar and the mock. It is not a new template.

A paid problem sometimes uses the same pattern as the chapter. That problem is a premium cousin. The chapter names the cousin in one sentence. The sentence tells you the twist. The cousin is not required work. You can finish this book without a paid account.

What to do on a study day

Work on one chapter for the day. Do these steps in order.

Read the chapter once. Include the worked example. Follow each step of the example on paper, with the same numbers the chapter uses. Read the example before you study the template.

Close the page. Open a blank file. Rewrite the template from memory. Write the whole template before you open the chapter again.

Open the chapter and check the update order. The update order is which variable changes on which line, and which of those lines runs first. If your order does not match, close the page and write the template again. Repeat until the update order matches.

Solve the Stay problems in the listed order. Finish Stay before you open the next chapter.

The Then problems come after Stay. Solve them before you leave the chapter. Stay plus Then is the first pass for that chapter. Second pass is not part of this day.

When you are stuck

Set a timer for 25 minutes. Use that time to name the pattern and to write the first lines of the template. Test a plan on a small input, even if you expect the plan to fail. A failed plan tells you which fact you missed.

If the 25 minutes end and you still have no correct approach, stop. Go back to the chapter. Reread only the section headed How you recognise it, and the section headed What to say out loud. Do not reread the whole chapter. Do not open a solution.

You are training recognition, not transcription. Recognition means you look at a new prompt and name the pattern. Transcription means you copy steps from a solution that is already open. Copying does not teach the name. In the interview the prompt is new, and no solution sits beside you. After those two sections, close the page and try the problem again from a blank file.

A 45-minute interview

A 45-minute interview has four beats. Do them in order. Say each beat out loud while you work.

First, restate the input and the output. Say what the function receives. Say what it must return. Say the limits in the prompt: how long the list can be, and whether a value can be missing, repeated, or negative. If you cannot say this in a few sentences, you do not know the problem yet. Wait to write code until you can.

Second, say the pattern name and the brute-force cost. Brute force means the direct plan that checks every possibility, such as every pair or every subarray. Say that plan's cost out loud. A common cost is O(n²). O(n²) means the work grows like n times n. If the list can be very long, say that this plan is too slow, and name the pattern you will use instead.

Third, write the template. While you write, narrate the invariant. An invariant is a fact that stays true every time the loop finishes a step. Point at the variables that fact uses. You might say that a running total covers every position from the first value through the value you just read, and that the same fact is true again after the next step. Say that fact. Leave the brackets and the semicolons unspoken.

Fourth, test four inputs before you say you are done. Use four inputs the constraints allow. The four are an empty list, one element, duplicates, and negatives. If a constraint forbids one of them, say that out loud, and replace it with a case the prompt does allow. Walk each input by hand. Check the position you read and the value you return. After those four checks, you may say you are done.

JavaScript you will trip on

The code in this book is the JavaScript you write in an interview. Six language facts can still make a correct pattern fail. Read them before chapter 0. An index is a position in a list. The first index is 0.

  1. Sort needs a comparator. A comparator is a function you pass to sort. It tells sort which of two values comes first. Take the list [10, 2, 1]. If you call sort and you pass no function, JavaScript turns the values into strings. String order is dictionary order. The character 1 comes before the characters 10, and the characters 10 come before the character 2, because the first character decides. The list becomes [1, 10, 2]. That is not number order. When the values are numbers, write sort((a, b) => a - b). A negative result puts a first. The list then becomes [1, 2, 10].
  2. Map versus object. An object key is a string. If you use a number as a key, JavaScript stores the characters of that number, not the number itself. A stored index of 0 is also falsy. Falsy means a test treats the value as false. So if (obj[need]) is false when the stored index is 0. You miss index 0, and you think the key is absent. The key is there. The test is wrong. Use a Map when the key is a number, and when the stored value might be 0. Map.has tells you the key is present even when the value is 0.
  3. Negative remainder. In JavaScript, -1 % 5 is -1, not 4. The % operator keeps the sign of the number on the left. A remainder you use as an index has to sit in the range from 0 up to one less than the divisor. The repair is ((x % k) + k) % k. For x = -1 and k = 5, the inner step is -1 + 5, which is 4, and 4 % 5 is 4. Chapter 8 uses this repair.
  4. Shared row array. Array(m).fill(Array(n).fill(0)) looks like a grid of zeros. It is not m separate rows. fill puts the same inner array in every row. A write on row 0 shows up on row 1, because every row is that same array. Chapter 18 shows the fix. Use Array.from, and build a new array for each row inside the function you pass. Each row is then its own array. A write on row 0 stays on row 0.
  5. Do not walk an array with for...in. for...in walks keys. It does not walk values. The keys are strings, so you get the characters 0 and 1, not the numbers 0 and 1. A prototype can show up in that walk. A prototype is a shared object behind the array. If a property was added there, the loop can visit it. Use a counted loop when you need the index: for (let i = 0; i < nums.length; i++). Use for...of when you only need each value.
  6. shift and unshift inside a loop are not free. shift removes the front value and slides every later value one place left. unshift inserts at the front and slides every value one place right. One call walks every later value. Do that n times and the cost is O(n²), the same n-times-n cost named above. A head index is the cheap version of removing from the front. You leave the array in place. You keep an integer at the front, and you add one to that integer when the front value is done. The next read starts at the new integer.

Links

Every practice link is a real LeetCode problem. The line shows the number and the official title, which is the title on the LeetCode page. The address has this shape: leetcode.com/problems/<slug>/. The slug is the short name in the address. It is lowercase, and a hyphen stands where the title has a space.

You can check the shape on a problem from chapter 0. Problem 1929 is Concatenation of Array. Its address is leetcode.com/problems/concatenation-of-array/. The number, the title, and the slug all name that same problem. If the page title does not match the ladder, you are on the wrong problem.

The difficulty tag matches the site. LeetCode rates a problem Easy, Medium, or Hard, and this book uses that same rating. The book does not retag a Hard problem as Easy.

The lane sits next to the difficulty, and the two tags answer different questions. The difficulty tag is LeetCode's rating. The lane is this book's order: Stay, Then, or Second pass. Open the ladder from the top. Solve in that order.

Carry forward. Chapter 0 is the list itself. An index is a position. The first index is 0. If the list holds n values, the last index you may read is n − 1. The next index, n, is past the end. The same chapter shows what O(n²) costs when n is 100,000. O(n²) means the work grows like n times n. 100,000 times 100,000 is 10 billion steps. At a rough speed of 100 million simple steps a second, that is about 100 seconds. A judge stops a solution before then. Two full loops are too slow for a list that long.

Chapter 00 · builds on nothing but counting

The array in JavaScript

An index is a position. The first is 0, the last is n − 1, and a subarray has no gaps.

What an array is

An array is a row of values stored in order. You reach a value by its index. An index is the position number of a slot. The first index is 0, not 1. If the array holds n values, the last index you may read is n − 1. nums.length is n. nums[nums.length] is one past the end. JavaScript gives you undefined there. It does not throw.

Take [10, 20, 30]. Here n is 3. Index 0 holds 10. Index 1 holds 20. Index 2 holds 30. Index 3 is not a slot. A loop that uses i <= nums.length reads that missing slot. On this array the bad read is nums[3].

Subarray, subsequence, subset

Interviews use three words for a part of an array, and the words are not interchangeable. A subarray is a contiguous slice: the chosen values sit next to each other, and their order stays. A subsequence keeps that order, but gaps are allowed. A subset is only a selection: order does not matter, and the empty selection counts. Take [2, 7, 3].

WordRuleThe list
SubarrayContiguous. Order stays.[2], [2,7], [2,7,3], [7], [7,3], [3]
SubsequenceOrder stays. Gaps allowed.The six subarrays, plus [2,3]
SubsetA selection. Order does not matter.{2,3} is one subset. Empty counts. 8 subsets.

The six subarrays are every contiguous block. There are n × (n + 1) / 2 of them. Here n is 3, so there are 6. [2, 3] skips 7, so it is not a subarray. It is a subsequence, because 2 still comes before 3 in the original row. Every subarray is a subsequence. The extra subsequences are the ones with gaps.

A subset does not care about order. {2, 3} is the same subset as {3, 2}. With three distinct values you can include or exclude each one, so there are 2³ = 8 subsets, and one of those eight is empty. If a prompt says subarray and you allow a gap, you will count pairs the problem does not want.

In place

In place means a handful of extra variables, not a second array whose length grows with n. Building an answer array of length n is allowed by many problems, and that build is not in place. The usual in-place contract changes the input array and returns a length k. The first k slots hold the answer. The caller reads only those slots. The rest may be garbage.

Cost, in one sitting

Write the cost before you write the loops. Interview arrays often have n up to 1e5, sometimes 1e6. A modern judge runs very roughly 1e8 simple operations a second. n² at n = 1e5 is 1e10 operations. That is about a hundred seconds. It will time out. n log n at the same n is about 1.7e6 operations. That is fine.

n up toFitsDoes not fit
100n³, sometimes 2ⁿ with pruningn⁴
1,000n²n³
1e5n log n, or nn²
1e6n, or n log n if the work inside is tinyn²

Read the table as a gate. When n is about 100, three nested loops can finish, and a pruned exponential search sometimes finishes. Four nested loops do not. When n is about 1,000, two loops fit and three do not. When n is 1e5, you need one pass, or one pass after a sort. Two full nested loops time out. When n is 1e6, even the work inside an n log n loop has to stay small.

Empty and one element

Empty and one element are part of the template, not a special puzzle. If you read nums[0], decide what an empty array returns before you write the loop. A single element has no neighbor. A loop that looks at index i and index i − 1 must start at 1, or the read of index −1 is undefined. If a single value is a valid run by itself, record a run of length 1. On the input [1], a consecutive-ones count that never updates on a lone 1 returns 0, and the right answer is 1.

The calls you will actually make

You will add and remove at the ends, and you will sort. push and pop touch the back. shift and unshift touch the front, and every later cell slides. The front operations are the expensive ones.

Sort is lexicographic until you pass a comparator.

const a = [10, 2, 1];
a.push(7);     // end, O(1) amortized. a is [10, 2, 1, 7]
a.pop();       // end, O(1). a is [10, 2, 1]
a.shift();     // front, O(n). everyone slides left
a.unshift(4);  // front, O(n). everyone slides right

const buggy = [10, 2, 1];
buggy.sort();                 // lexicographic: [1, 10, 2]
const fixed = [10, 2, 1];
fixed.sort((x, y) => x - y);  // numeric: [1, 2, 10]

push(7) puts 7 at the end in amortized constant time. The array grows sometimes, and those rare copies are spread across many pushes. pop() removes that end in constant time. shift() removes index 0, and every remaining value moves one slot left, so one shift is O(n). unshift(4) slides everyone right to open index 0. It is also O(n). If you shift inside a loop, n times, the cost is about n². On n = 1e5 that times out.

The default sort() does not compare numbers. It turns each value into a string and sorts those strings in dictionary order. Dictionary order means the characters decide, from the left. Take [10, 2, 1]. The strings are "10", "2", and "1". "1" comes before "10" because both start with the character 1, and the shorter string ends first. "10" comes before "2" because the character 1 comes before the character 2. The array becomes [1, 10, 2]. Ten is sitting between 1 and 2.

Any time the values are numbers, pass a comparator. The form you write is (a, b) => a - b. If a is 10 and b is 2, the result is 8, which is positive, so a is placed after b. If the result is negative, a comes first. If the result is 0, those two values keep their current order. The block above uses the names x and y. The subtraction is the same comparison. The same bug hides when you sort intervals later. Sort by a[0] - b[0], not by the default string order.

Two names can be one array

An array is an object. A variable holds a reference, which is a name for that one array. Assignment copies the name. It does not copy the slots.

Assignment copies the reference.

const a = [1, 2];
const b = a;
b[0] = 9;          // a[0] is 9 as well
const copy = a.slice();
copy[0] = 0;       // a is unchanged

a starts as [1, 2]. b = a copies the reference, so b and a name the same row. b[0] = 9 writes into that row. a[0] is 9 as well. slice() copies one level: copy is a new array holding the same numbers. copy[0] = 0 changes the copy. a stays [9, 2]. The spread [...a] is the same kind of copy. It is shallow. Shallow means one level. If a cell holds another array, that inner array is still shared.

That fact becomes a real bug when you build a matrix. Array(m).fill(Array(n).fill(0)) builds one inner array and stores that same reference in every row. Change one row and every row changes. Chapter 18 finishes the bug and shows the repair. Until then, remember the rule from this block: slice or [...a] copies one level, and each row of a matrix needs its own inner array.

Plus one, because digits are an array

You can store a number as an array of digits, with the most significant digit at index 0. Plus one adds 1 to the last digit. When a digit is 9, that slot becomes 0 and the 1 carries left.

Take [1, 2, 9]. Start at the last index, i = 2. The digit is 9. 9 is not less than 9, so you cannot add 1 in that slot. Write 0 and keep the carry. The array is now [1, 2, 0]. Move to i = 1. The digit is 2, and 2 is less than 9. Add 1. The slot becomes 3. Return at once. The answer is [1, 3, 0]. Index 0 stays 1, because the carry died at index 1. You never look at a slot after the return.

Take [9, 9]. At i = 1 the digit is 9, so write 0 and carry. The array is [9, 0]. At i = 0 the digit is 9 again, so write 0 and carry. The array is [0, 0]. The next index would be −1, so the loop ends, and the carry is still alive. There is no digit left to receive it. unshift(1) puts that 1 at the front and slides the old digits right once. The answer is [1, 0, 0]. You do that extra slide at most once, so the whole function is still linear in the number of digits.

Plus one. [9, 9] must become [1, 0, 0].

function plusOne(digits) {
  for (let i = digits.length - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
    if (digits[i] < 9) {
      digits[i] += 1;
      return digits;
    }
    digits[i] = 0;
  }
  digits.unshift(1);
  return digits;
}

The loop starts at the last index and walks toward 0. That direction is the carry. The test digits[i] < 9 happens before any write of 0. When the test passes, you add 1 and return, so a later slot is left alone. When the test fails, you write 0 and the loop itself is the carry. If the loop finishes, unshift(1) is the leading digit. Doing that unshift on every step would be a different, slower algorithm. Here it runs only after every digit was 9.

[1, 2, 9] i=2: 9, write 0, carry on i=1: 2, write 3, return [1, 3, 0] [9, 9] both become 0, loop ends, unshift 1 → [1, 0, 0]

One pass from the right. O(n) time. The final unshift moves n digits once, which is still O(n).

A rotated sorted array, counted by drops

A non-decreasing array can be cut, and the front piece can be moved to the back. That shape is a rotation. It has at most one drop. A drop is a place where a value is greater than the next value. The wrap from the last index back to index 0 counts as a pair.

Take [3, 4, 5, 1, 2]. There are five values, so the last index is 4. Start a drop count at 0. Index 0 holds 3 and the next cell holds 4. 3 is not greater than 4, so the count stays 0. Index 1 holds 4 and the next holds 5. Still no drop. Index 2 holds 5 and the next holds 1. 5 is greater than 1, so the count becomes 1. That pair is the cut. Index 3 holds 1 and the next holds 2. No drop. Index 4 holds 2. The next index is (4 + 1) mod 5, which is 0, and that cell holds 3. 2 is not greater than 3, so the wrap is not a second drop. The count is 1. One drop is allowed, so this array passes.

Now take [3, 5, 1, 4, 2]. Index 1 holds 5 and the next holds 1, which is a drop. Index 3 holds 4 and the next holds 2, which is another drop. Two drops means this is not a rotation of a non-decreasing array. The function returns false. Equal neighbors are not drops, so [1, 1, 1] has a count of 0 and passes. A fully sorted array also has a count of 0 and passes. The test is drops <= 1, which allows zero or one. You will not binary-search this shape until chapter 14. Counting the drops is enough here.

At most one descent, including the wrap.

function check(nums) {
  let drops = 0;
  for (let i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
    const next = nums[(i + 1) % nums.length];
    if (nums[i] > next) drops += 1;
  }
  return drops <= 1;
}

drops starts at 0 so a sorted array can still succeed. The loop index runs from 0 through n − 1. (i + 1) % nums.length is what makes the last step read index 0. You add 1 only when nums[i] > next. A tie is allowed, because non-decreasing includes equals. The return uses <=. If you required drops === 1, the input [1, 2, 3] would fail, and it is already sorted.

Mistakes

  • On [10, 2, 1], a loop condition i <= nums.length reads index 3. That read is undefined. A later numeric step becomes NaN, or a comparison quietly does nothing.
  • On [10, 2, 1], sort() with no comparator returns [1, 10, 2]. Any later scan that assumes 10 is the largest value is wrong.
  • Building a second array of length n and calling the algorithm in place is the wrong cost. The extra memory grows with n. In place means a handful of variables.
  • for...in on an array walks keys, and those keys are strings. A property sitting on the prototype can show up as a fake index. Use an index loop or for...of.
  • Calling shift once per element on an array of length n slides the tail n times. The input that shows the cost is a long row, n = 1e5. The time is about n², and the judge times out.

Practice

Stay problems are the mechanics of indexes, copies, and counts. Then problems are those same mechanics with a carry, a digit count, or a wrap.

Stay before chapter 1. Then before you leave the chapter.

  1. 1929. Concatenation of Array easy Stay

    Build an array of length 2n. ans[i] and ans[i + n] both read nums[i].

  2. 1920. Build Array from Permutation easy Stay

    ans[i] = nums[nums[i]]. Two reads. Do not confuse the index with the value.

  3. 1470. Shuffle the Array easy Stay

    The array is x1..xn y1..yn. Place them as x1,y1,x2,y2 by writing index 2*i and 2*i+1.

  4. 1512. Number of Good Pairs easy Stay

    A pair is two indices i < j with the same value. Count frequencies, then add c*(c-1)/2 for each count.

  5. 1431. Kids With the Greatest Number of Candies easy Then

    One pass for the max, a second pass to compare candies[i] + extraCandies with that max.

  6. 1295. Find Numbers with Even Number of Digits easy Then

    Count digits by dividing by 10, or by the length of the decimal string. Zero is one digit.

  7. 66. Plus One easy Then

    The template above. The only new digit is a leading 1 when every digit was 9.

  8. 989. Add to Array-Form of Integer easy Then

    Add k from the right. The carry is Math.floor(sum / 10), and k shrinks the same way.

  9. 1752. Check if Array Is Sorted and Rotated easy Then

    Count strict drops, including the pair (last, first). The answer is drops <= 1.

Rewrite from memory

Close the page and write the comparator (a, b) => a - b, the plus-one loop on [9, 9], and the six subarrays of [2, 7, 3] from memory.

Carry forward. Chapter 1 uses index, subarray, and in place to classify a prompt before you choose a loop.

Chapter 01 · builds on chapter 0

How to read an array problem

Four lines before any code: the input, the output, whether the answer is contiguous or in place, and what the brute force costs.

You can already name an index, a subarray, and an in-place write. An index is a position, and the first one is 0. A subarray is a contiguous slice. In place means a handful of extra variables, not a second array of length n. The next skill is classification. You refuse to code until four lines on paper tell you which later chapter the prompt belongs to.

The 25-minute habit lives in the study chapter, the page before chapter 0. When you are stuck, that page tells you to set a timer and then reread the recognition for the chapter you already chose. This chapter does not use that timer. The skill here is the choice itself. You name the shape. You do not reach for a clever loop yet.

The four lines

Write four lines before any code. Each line is one question. Chapter 3 is the brute force those lines point at. Chapters 4 through 16 exist because the fourth line often says the brute force is too slow. You do not need those chapters memorized today. You need the four lines, so the chapter number is a conclusion.

Input. What is n, roughly? Are values sorted? Can they be negative? Can they be zero? n is the length. If n can be 1e5, two nested loops will not finish, which you already priced in chapter 0. If the values are sorted, a later chapter can throw away one side after a single comparison. If negatives are allowed, a running total can fall and then recover, and a band that only shrinks can miss an answer. Zero matters when you count digits, multiply signs, or divide. Write the answers you actually have, including "negatives are forbidden" when the prompt says so.

Output. A boolean, an index, a length, a count of subarrays, or a rebuilt array? A boolean often means you only need to know that something has been seen. An index means you must store a position, and index 0 is a real position, not a missing one. A length paired with an in-place write means the caller reads a prefix of the same array. A prefix is the contiguous block from index 0 through some index. A count of subarrays means you are visiting slices, not only pairs. A rebuilt array of length n is extra memory the problem asked for. Building it is not a failure, and it is not in place.

Shape. Does the answer have to be contiguous? Does it have to be in place? Contiguous means a subarray: the values sit next to each other and the order stays. A subsequence keeps order and allows gaps. A subset ignores order. If the prompt says subarray and you allow a gap, the loops are wrong before they start. If it says in place, a second array of length n breaks the contract even when the values are right. Chapter 0 fixed these words. Use them in this line.

Brute cost. If you tried every pair, every subarray, or every possible answer, what is the cost, and does the constraint allow it? Pairs are about n². Subarrays with a fresh sum inside are also about n². Trying every possible speed one by one is often too slow, even when a later chapter can search those speeds in order. Write the Big-O before you nest a loop. If that cost fits the table from chapter 0, the brute force is the answer. If it does not fit, the four lines have told you why a later chapter exists. They have not told you to guess its name from a keyword.

How a prompt points at a later chapter

After the four lines, you still say the direction in a full sentence. You are not choosing syntax. You are naming the family the lines imply.

  • If the value you need at each index is a sum, a count, a minimum, or a maximum of what you have already seen, you are heading toward one pass.
  • If the answer is one contiguous band and a running total is enough to accept or reject that band, you are heading toward one pass, a window, or a prefix.
  • If any pair will do, order does not matter, and the values are not sorted, you are heading toward a map, or toward a sort.
  • If the array is already sorted and each comparison lets you throw away one side, you are heading toward two indexes that move inward.
  • If n is a few hundred and the prompt asks you to count every pair or every triple, you are heading toward two loops, and that cost is allowed.
  • If the prompt asks for the minimum speed, capacity, or day such that a plan works, you are heading toward a search over the answer, not a search over an index.

Use chapter 2 when the answer at index i needs only the current value and a short summary of one side.

Use chapter 3 when n is small and every pair, every triple, or every subarray is cheap enough to write directly.

Use chapter 7 when every value that the condition cares about is positive, the answer is a contiguous band, and shrinking the left end is what repairs the band.

Use chapter 8 when you count subarrays with a given sum and a negative value can repair a sum you have already walked past.

Use chapter 14 when you are searching a speed, a capacity, or a day, and once a candidate works every larger candidate also works.

Use chapter 15 when each index wants the nearest later value that is strictly greater, such as the next warmer day.

What to do with the eight drills

For each prompt below, write the chapter number you think it will land in, and one sentence that quotes your four lines. Do not write code. The answer key is in chapter 17. Leave that chapter closed until all eight guesses are on paper. If you open it first, you skip the skill this chapter is teaching. After each guess, check it against the brute-cost line. If that cost already fits, do not reach for a later chapter just because the later chapter sounds advanced.

  1. Longest subarray whose sum is at most K. Every value is positive.
  2. Count the subarrays whose sum is exactly K. Values may be negative.
  3. Two indices in a sorted array add to a target.
  4. Remove duplicates from a sorted array, in place.
  5. For each day, how many days until a strictly warmer temperature.
  6. How many islands are in a grid of land and water.
  7. Add a value on many index ranges, then read the final array.
  8. The minimum eating speed so the piles are finished within H hours.

The wording above is the whole prompt you get. You fill in n and the shape yourself, the way the four lines ask. A good sentence names contiguous or in place when the prompt used that idea, and it names a cost. Chapter 17 will show the landing chapter. Your job in this chapter is to be able to defend the guess from the four lines.

Mistakes

  • The failure starts when a prompt looks like one you have seen, and you code before the four lines. A familiar word such as maximum sends you into a stock loop. On a later input whose values may be negative, that loop tracks a minimum price and returns 0, while the asked answer was a different kind of maximum.
  • Calling every contiguous problem a sliding window fails on a count of subarrays whose sum is exactly K when negatives are allowed. A window that only shrinks cannot be repaired by a later negative, so it under-counts. Chapter 7 and chapter 8 exist so you can tell those prompts apart.
  • Guessing the chapter from one keyword fails in both directions. The word maximum by itself is not a reason to use the best-subarray-sum loop. The word sorted by itself is not a reason to binary-search. The four lines are what decide.

Rewrite from memory

Close the page and write the four lines, then classify the eight drills again without looking at chapter 17.

Carry forward. Chapter 2 is the first algorithm: one pass, where the answer at i depends only on the current value and a summary of what you have already seen.

Chapter 02 · builds on chapter 1

One pass and running state

The answer at i is a function of the current value and a summary of the prefix you have already walked.

What this pattern is

One pass means you visit each index once. You walk left to right, or you walk right to left. You do not start the array over. A running state is a small summary of the indexes you have already visited. The summary might be a sum, a count, a minimum, or a maximum. At the current index you may use the value in that cell and the summary. You may not rescan the earlier cells.

An index is the position of a cell. The first index is 0. A prefix is the contiguous block from index 0 through the index you have just finished. An invariant is a sentence about your variables that stays true every time you finish an index. Here the invariant is: the summary describes exactly the prefix you have walked, and the answer at this index is a function of that summary and the current value.

Picture a walk down a street of shops. You do not memorize every price. You remember the cheapest price so far, which is one number. At the next shop you ask one question with that memory, and only then do you update the memory. The array is the street. The running state is the note in your pocket.

What you already know

Chapter 0 gave you indexes, the cost table, and the meaning of in place. Chapter 1 gave you four lines so you can name this family before you code. This chapter adds the first algorithm. The answer at i depends only on nums[i] and a summary of one side. If the summary would have to drop a cell in the middle and remember it again later, you are past this chapter.

A worked example

Take the array [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]. It holds five values, so the last index is 4. Index 0 holds 1. You keep one number, called sum, and it starts at 0. That 0 is the sum of the empty prefix, before any cell is included. At index 0 you add 1, so sum becomes 1, and you record 1. You add before you record, because this cell has to be inside the sum you store. At index 1 the value is 2. Sum becomes 3, and you record 3. You do not read index 0 again, because that 1 is already inside sum. At index 2 the value is 3 and sum becomes 6. At index 3 the value is 4 and sum becomes 10. At index 4 the value is 5 and sum becomes 15. The recorded array is [1, 3, 6, 10, 15].

After each record, sum equals the last recorded value, and the recorded list is exactly the prefix sums of the cells you have walked. Nothing to the right of the current index has been added. If you recorded sum before adding the current value, index 0 would store 0, and the final 15 would never be written. On the smaller input [1, 2] that swapped order returns [0, 1] instead of [1, 3].

[1, 2, 3, 4, 5] index 0: sum 1, record [1] index 1: sum 3, record [1, 3] index 2: sum 6, record [1, 3, 6] index 3: sum 10, record [1, 3, 6, 10] index 4: sum 15, record [1, 3, 6, 10, 15]

How you recognise it

  • The value you need at i is a sum, a count, a minimum, or a maximum of the prefix you have walked, and you do not need the exact earlier index that produced it.
  • One buy and one later sell can be answered from the cheapest price seen so far, as long as you sell against the old minimum.
  • A run of equal values, or of strictly increasing values, resets when the rule breaks, and you only keep the current length and the best length.
  • The answer at i is the greatest value strictly to the right, so one walk from the last index can carry that best value.
  • A balance of the left sum and the right sum becomes arithmetic once a first pass has computed the total.
  • If the correct summary would have to forget a cell that fell out of a moving band, this chapter is the wrong tool.

Use chapter 7 when the answer is one contiguous band and dropping the left end is what repairs the band.

Use chapter 11 when you want the best sum of any contiguous segment and a negative prefix should be thrown away.

Use chapter 15 when you need the nearest greater value, not the greatest value anywhere to the right.

What to say out loud

I walk each index once. When I finish index i, the summary matches the prefix I have walked, and it does not include anything past i. The answer uses that summary together with the current value. If the answer needs the old summary, I use the old summary before I change it.

The template

The summary is the sum so far.

function runningSum(nums) {
  const out = [];
  let sum = 0;
  for (const x of nums) {
    sum += x;
    out.push(sum);
  }
  return out;
}

The invariant is: after each push, out holds the prefix sums of the values already seen, and sum equals the last of those prefix sums. out starts empty and sum starts at 0, which is the empty prefix. Inside the loop you add x and only then push. That order is the off-by-one. Pushing first stores the previous prefix in the current slot and drops the final sum on the floor. The loop has no index, so you cannot mis-count a bound. You push once per value, so the output length equals the input length. An empty input never enters the loop and returns an empty array.

Highest altitude is this same loop on a list of gains. The altitude starts at 0, which counts as a place you have touched. Add each gain into a running sum, and keep the largest altitude you have seen, including the start. Take gains [-5, 1, 5, 0, -7]. The altitudes are 0, then −5, then −4, then 1, then 1, then −6. The best is 1. The final altitude is −6, and that final number is not the answer. A negative gain can pull you down after the best point.

A run that resets

Take [1, 1, 0, 1, 1, 1]. You want the longest contiguous block of ones. run is the current block. best is the longest block finished so far. Both start at 0. At index 0 the value is 1, so run becomes 1 and best becomes 1. At index 1 the value is 1, so run becomes 2 and best becomes 2. At index 2 the value is 0. A zero ends the block, so run goes back to 0. Best stays 2, because the earlier block is still the champion. At index 3 the value is 1 and run becomes 1. Best stays 2, because 1 is not longer. At index 4 run becomes 2. At index 5 run becomes 3, and best becomes 3. The answer is 3. The zero threw away the current run only.

On the input [1] the same steps set run to 1 and best to 1. A single 1 is a real block. If best stayed 0 until a zero arrived, this input would return 0.

The summary is the current run. A zero throws it away.

function findMaxConsecutiveOnes(nums) {
  let best = 0;
  let run = 0;
  for (const x of nums) {
    if (x === 1) {
      run += 1;
      if (run > best) best = run;
    } else {
      run = 0;
    }
  }
  return best;
}

On a 1 you add to run first, then you compare with best. On a 0 you set run to 0 and you do not touch best. Updating best before the increment would store a length that is one short. Resetting best on a zero would forget the earlier champion. The invariant is: best is the longest run entirely inside the prefix you have finished, and run is the length of the unfinished block that touches the current index, or 0 if the current value broke it.

[1, 1, 0, 1, 1, 1] run 1, 2, then 0 resets, then 1, 2, 3 best = 3

The same loop changes only the reset rule. A longest strict increase resets the run to 1, not to 0, when the value does not grow, because the current cell still starts a new block of length 1. A single element is length 1. Consecutive characters is the same run, compared on letters. A large group remembers the start index of the current character, and when the run ends you record that start and end if the length is at least 3. A mountain is a strict climb and then a strict descent. The peak cannot be index 0 or the last index, and the walk has to finish on the last index. A monotonic check keeps two flags, still non-decreasing and still non-increasing. An equal neighbor leaves both flags alive. The sign of a product is not a multiplication. A zero returns 0. Otherwise you count the negatives, and the sign is the parity of that count.

Best day to sell

Take prices [7, 1, 5, 3, 6, 4]. There are six days. You may buy once and sell once, and the sell index must be later than the buy index. minPrice is the cheapest earlier price. It starts at infinity, so no fake buy exists yet. best starts at 0, because skipping the trade is allowed. At index 0 the price is 7. Profit against infinity is not a gain, so best stays 0. Only after that check does 7 become the minimum. At index 1 the price is 1. Profit is 1 − 7, a loss, so best stays 0. Then 1 is cheaper than 7, so the minimum becomes 1. You used the old minimum, 7, before you stored 1. At index 2 the price is 5. Profit is 5 − 1 = 4, so best becomes 4. The price 5 is not a new minimum. At index 3 the price is 3. Profit is 2, which does not beat 4. At index 4 the price is 6. Profit is 6 − 1 = 5, so best becomes 5. At index 5 the price is 4. Profit is 3, and best stays 5. The answer is 5. That sale buys at index 1 and sells at index 4.

Profit uses yesterday's minimum. Then the minimum may move.

function maxProfit(prices) {
  let minPrice = Infinity;
  let best = 0;
  for (const price of prices) {
    const profit = price - minPrice;
    if (profit > best) best = profit;
    if (price < minPrice) minPrice = price;
  }
  return best;
}

The invariant is: best is the best sale on a day you have already finished, and minPrice is the cheapest price strictly before any sale you might make tomorrow. Infinity at the start means the first day has no earlier buy. The profit line reads minPrice before the update line writes it. That order is the whole algorithm. The off-by-one is not an index bound. It is those two if statements. If the minimum moves first, today is both the buy and the sell, and today's profit is 0.

[1, 2] correct: price 1, profit is -Infinity so best stays 0, then min = 1. price 2, profit = 1, best = 1. broken, if you write the min first: price 1, min = 1, profit = 0 price 2, min = 2, profit = 0 answer 0, and you can never sell above the min you just stored.

The greatest value on the right

Sometimes the summary lives to the right of i. Take [17, 18, 5, 4, 6, 1]. You replace each cell with the greatest value strictly to its right. The last cell has nothing to its right, so its answer is −1. Start best at −1, and start the index at 5. Save 1, write −1 into index 5, then let best become 1 because 1 is greater than −1. At index 4 save 6, write 1, then best becomes 6. At index 3 save 4, write 6, and leave best at 6 because 4 is smaller. At index 2 save 5, write 6, and leave best at 6. At index 1 save 18, write 6, then best becomes 18. At index 0 save 17, write 18, and leave best at 18 because 17 is smaller. The array is [18, 6, 6, 6, 1, -1].

This is the greatest value anywhere to the right. It is not the nearest greater value. A smaller neighbor can still be the nearest greater one, and one running maximum cannot remember that wait. That wait is chapter 15.

Replace, then consider the old value as a candidate.

function replaceElements(arr) {
  let best = -1;
  for (let i = arr.length - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
    const cur = arr[i];
    arr[i] = best;
    if (cur > best) best = cur;
  }
  return arr;
}

You save cur before the write. Then you store best into the cell. Only then may cur raise best. If you raised best first, the cell would be replaced by a maximum that includes itself. The last index would become its own value instead of −1. If you wrote the cell before saving cur, the old value would be gone. On [17, 18, 5, 4, 6, 1] the 1 at index 5 has to be saved before −1 overwrites it, or best never becomes 1 and every earlier answer shifts. The loop bound is i >= 0. The step i-- is what moves you left. best starts at −1 so the rightmost write has a defined answer.

[17, 18, 5, 4, 6, 1] from the right, best starts at -1 1 → write -1, best = 1 6 → write 1, best = 6 4 → write 6 5 → write 6 18 → write 6, best = 18 17 → write 18 result [18, 6, 6, 6, 1, -1]

The pivot index

A pivot index has an equal sum on both sides. The pivot value itself belongs to neither side. You cannot know the right-hand sum until you know the total, so the first walk only adds. Take [1, 7, 3, 6, 5, 6]. The total is 28. left starts at 0. At index 0, right is 28 − 0 − 1 = 27. 0 is not 27, so now left becomes 1. At index 1, right is 28 − 1 − 7 = 20. Not equal, so left becomes 8. At index 2, right is 28 − 8 − 3 = 17. Not equal, so left becomes 11. At index 3, right is 28 − 11 − 6 = 11. The two sides match, so you return 3. You return on the match, before left absorbs 6. Indexes 4 and 5 are never needed once 3 has matched. If no index matches, you return −1.

The total turns one future sum into arithmetic.

function pivotIndex(nums) {
  let total = 0;
  for (const x of nums) total += x;
  let left = 0;
  for (let i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
    const right = total - left - nums[i];
    if (left === right) return i;
    left += nums[i];
  }
  return -1;
}

The first loop finishes the total before any comparison. In the second loop the order is fixed. Compute right from the old left and from nums[i]. Compare. Only then add nums[i] into left. The pivot is in neither sum, which is why it is subtracted out of the right-hand side and not yet added to the left. If you add first, index 3 on this input has left 17 and right 5, the match disappears, and you return −1. An empty array skips both loops and returns −1. A one-element array has left 0 and right 0, so index 0 is a pivot.

[1, 7, 3, 6, 5, 6], total 28 i=0 left 0, right 27, no, left becomes 1 i=1 left 1, right 20, no, left becomes 8 i=2 left 8, right 17, no, left becomes 11 i=3 left 11, right 11, return 3

The total is the sum of the whole array. The right-hand sum is that total, minus the left prefix, minus the pivot cell. This is the bridge into prefix sums. Chapter 8 will name the prefix array. Here two numbers are enough.

Time and extra memory

Every walk in this chapter visits each index a constant number of times, so the time is O(n). Extra memory is O(1), except the running sum, which allocates the output array the problem asks for.

Mistakes

  • If you update the minimum price before the profit, the input [1, 2] returns 0. Day 0 stores 1 and records profit 0. Day 1 stores 2 before the subtraction, so the profit is 0 again. The sale of 2 against the old minimum 1 is never computed. The right answer is 1.
  • If a run-length answer stays 0 until a reset, the input [1] returns 0. A single 1 is a run of length 1. The best has to move on that lone 1.
  • If you overwrite a cell and then read it as the greatest value on the right, the input [17, 18, 5, 4, 6, 1] loses the 1 at the last index. Best never becomes 1, and the written answers are wrong. Save the old value, write the answer, then consider the old value.
  • If you add nums[i] into the left sum before the comparison, the input [1, 7, 3, 6, 5, 6] misses index 3. The pivot belongs to neither side. Adding 6 first makes the left side 17 and the right side 5, so you return −1.
  • If the running sum pushes before it adds, the input [1, 2] returns [0, 1]. Each slot holds the previous prefix, and the last sum is never stored.

Practice

Stay before chapter 3. Then before you leave the chapter.

  1. 1480. Running Sum of 1d Array easy Stay

    The running-sum template. out[i] is the sum of the prefix ending at i.

  2. 485. Max Consecutive Ones easy Stay

    Reset the run on 0. Track the best run. Empty input returns 0.

  3. 121. Best Time to Buy and Sell Stock easy Stay

    One buy, one sell, sell after buy. Profit before you move the minimum.

  4. 1299. Replace Elements with Greatest Element on Right Side easy Stay

    Walk from the right. The last answer is -1.

  5. 724. Find Pivot Index easy Stay

    Total first, then left versus total - left - nums[i]. Return the leftmost index.

  6. 1732. Find the Highest Altitude easy Then

    Running sum of the gains. The best altitude includes the start at 0.

  7. 674. Longest Continuous Increasing Subsequence easy Then

    Reset the run to 1 when the value does not strictly increase. A single element is length 1.

  8. 1446. Consecutive Characters easy Then

    The same run, compared on characters instead of bits.

  9. 830. Positions of Large Groups easy Then

    When a run of the same character ends, record [start, end] if the length is at least 3.

  10. 896. Monotonic Array easy Then

    Two flags: still non-decreasing, still non-increasing. Equals keep both flags alive.

  11. 941. Valid Mountain Array easy Then

    Strict climb, then strict descent. The peak cannot be the first or the last index.

  12. 1822. Sign of the Product of an Array easy Then

    A zero returns 0. Otherwise the sign is the parity of the negative count. Do not multiply.

  13. 1991. Find the Middle Index in Array easy Then

    The same balance as the pivot index. Left sum equals right sum.

Rewrite from memory

Rewrite the stock loop and the right-to-left replacement from a blank file, and keep the old minimum in use until the profit line has run.

Carry forward. Chapter 3 drops the summary and writes both loops, so you can see the O(n²) scan that later chapters replace.

Chapter 03 · builds on chapter 2

Two loops

When n is small, write the pairs or the subarrays directly. Reset the inner sum at every new start.

What this pattern is

Two loops means you name two indexes, and the inner index runs across the partners of the outer index. You use this when you truly need every pair, or every subarray, and n is small enough that n² fits. A pair is two indexes i and j with i before j. A subarray is a contiguous slice: you pick a start, then you extend the end one cell at a time. There is no running summary that can replace those visits. The visits are the algorithm.

An index is a position, and the first one is 0. An invariant here is small. When the outer index moves, the inner sum goes back to 0, so the new start does not inherit the previous start. Picture checking every window of a short shelf by hand. The shelf is short, so touching every window is honest work. When the shelf grows to 1e5 items, this chapter is the thing you time out on, and a later chapter has to replace it.

What you already know

Chapter 2 answered an index from a summary of one side. This chapter is what you write when a summary is the wrong tool and the cost table from chapter 0 says n² still fits. You already know a subarray has no gaps. This chapter adds the double loop that lists them, and the habit of resetting the inner sum at every new start.

A worked example

Take nums = [1, 2, 1, 2, 1] and k = 3. There are five values, so there are 5 × 6 / 2 = 15 subarrays, and the loops will touch all 15. Start i at 0 and set sum back to 0. j walks from 0. Sum becomes 1, then 3, which matches, so the count becomes 1. That subarray is indexes 0 through 1. Sum then becomes 4, then 6, then 7. None of those later sums is 3. Now i moves to 1. This is the step people skip. Sum is set back to 0 before j starts at 1. If you forgot that reset, sum would still be 7 and every later total would be nonsense. From i = 1 the sums are 2, then 3. The second 3 is indexes 1 through 2, and the count becomes 2. The longer sums from this start are 5 and 6.

i moves to 2, and sum is 0 again. j starts at 2. The first sum is 1. Then j = 3 makes sum 3, indexes 2 through 3, and the count becomes 3. The last extension is 4. i moves to 3. Sum starts at 0. Index 3 alone is 2. Indexes 3 through 4 sum to 3, and the count becomes 4. i moves to 4. The only sum is 1. The count stays 4. The four hits are [1, 2] at the start, [2, 1] next, [1, 2] starting at index 2, and [2, 1] starting at index 3.

A pair loop is a different inner start. j begins at i + 1, so you never compare a value with itself, and you never count (j, i) again after you have counted (i, j). The code below is the subarray version, which is why j begins at i and the sum is reset there.

nums = [1, 2, 1, 2, 1], k = 3 i=0 sums 1, 3 hit, 4, 6, 7 i=1 sums 2, 3 hit, 5, 6 i=2 sums 1, 3 hit, 4 i=3 sums 2, 3 hit i=4 sums 1 count = 4 subarrays in length n: n * (n + 1) / 2 n = 5 → 15, and the loops visit all 15

How you recognise it

  • n is a few hundred, or the prompt says to count every pair, and the cost table says n² fits.
  • You need each pair of indexes with the left index strictly before the right index.
  • You need each subarray, meaning each start i and each end j that is i or to the right of i.
  • Nothing is sorted, there is no complement you can store, and no end of a window is safe to discard.
  • Three indexes with i before j before k, and n at most about 100, is the same idea with one more loop.
  • If the inner question is only "have I already seen one special value", a later chapter removes the inner loop.

Use chapter 4 when the inner loop only asks whether you have already seen the complement of the current value.

Use chapter 8 when n is large and you count subarrays whose sum is k, including when negatives are allowed.

Use chapter 15 when the inner scan looks for the next smaller or greater value to the right of i.

What to say out loud

I will name the cost before I nest the loops. For pairs, the inner index starts at i + 1. For subarrays, the inner sum goes back to 0 at every new start, and the inner index starts at i. If that cost does not fit, I stop and I do not pretend the nested loops are the final answer.

The template

Reset the sum at every start. This is the algorithm chapter 8 replaces.

function countSubarraysWithSum(nums, k) {
  let count = 0;
  for (let i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
    let sum = 0;
    for (let j = i; j < nums.length; j++) {
      sum += nums[j];
      if (sum === k) count += 1;
    }
  }
  return count;
}

The invariant is: when j moves, sum is the sum of indexes i through j, and count includes every earlier subarray whose sum was k. let sum = 0 sits inside the outer loop, which is the reset. The inner loop starts at j = i, so a single cell is a subarray. You add nums[j] before you compare with k. Comparing first would test the previous end. The off-by-one is the inner start. j = 0 would revisit cells left of i and would count the same slice many times. j = i + 1 would skip every one-cell subarray.

Final prices with a discount, solved in this chapter, is the pair shape. For each i, scan j to the right for the first price that is less than or equal to prices[i]. Subtract that later price when you find it. The same question becomes a stack in chapter 15, because "the next smaller price" is the next-smaller problem. Write the pair version until you can feel which inner scans are only searching for a neighbor.

Time and extra memory

Pairs are O(n²) and subarrays visited this way are O(n²), with O(1) extra memory. Three nested index loops are O(n³). At n = 100 that is about a million steps and usually fits. At n = 1e5, n² does not.

Mistakes

  • If the inner pair loop starts at j = 0, the input [1, 2] counted as pairs with i before j becomes two visits, (0, 1) and (1, 0). You double-count, or you use a value twice when you forget to skip i === j.
  • If the subarray sum is not reset when i moves, the input [1, 1, 1] with k = 2 fails after the first start. The first start ends with sum 3. The second start adds onto 3 and never sees the subarray [1, 1] that begins at index 1. The count comes out short.
  • Reaching for a map on an input of length 50 still computes an answer, and it hides the double loop this chapter is teaching. The nested loops are the algorithm while n² fits. Use them until the fourth line of chapter 1 says they do not fit.

Practice

Stay before chapter 4. Then before you leave the chapter.

  1. 1365. How Many Numbers Are Smaller Than the Current Number easy Stay

    For each i, count j where nums[j] < nums[i]. n is at most 500. The pair loops are the point.

  2. 1588. Sum of All Odd Length Subarrays easy Stay

    Enumerate subarrays, add the sum when the length is odd. Later you can also count how many odd-length subarrays cover each index.

  3. 1534. Count Good Triplets easy Then

    Three loops, i < j < k, and three absolute-value checks. n is at most 100.

  4. 1475. Final Prices With a Special Discount in a Shop easy Then

    For each i, the first j > i with prices[j] <= prices[i]. Brute force now. The stack version is chapter 15.

Rewrite from memory

From a blank file, rewrite the double loop that counts subarrays with sum k, including the reset of sum inside the outer loop.

Carry forward. Chapter 4 takes the inner question "have I seen the complement?" and answers it with a map.

Chapter 04 · builds on chapters 2 and 3

Hash map

The inner loop asked whether you have seen the other value that finishes the pair. Look that up, then insert.

What this pattern is

A hash map stores a value under a key so you can ask for that key later without scanning. In this chapter the key is usually a number you have already seen. The stored thing is an index, a count, or a list. A complement is the other value that finishes a pair. If the target is T and the current value is x, the complement is T − x. The inner loop of chapter 3 often asked only "have I seen the complement?" The map answers that in expected constant time.

An index is a position, and 0 is a real index. An invariant is a sentence that stays true at the start of each loop step. Here it is: the map holds values from indexes strictly before i, and the current value is not in the map yet. You look up the complement first. You insert the current value after that lookup. Picture a coat check. You ask whether the matching ticket is already on the board. You hang your own coat after the question, so you cannot match a coat with itself.

What you already know

Chapter 2 kept a summary of one side. Chapter 3 answered "have I seen this?" by scanning. This chapter keeps the question and replaces the scan with a lookup. You still walk the array once. The new tool is the map, and the new rule is the order: lookup, then insert.

A worked example

Take nums = [8, 4, 5, 1, 7, 2] and target 10. There are six values. Use a Map. At index 0 the value is 8, so the complement is 2. The map is empty, so there is no hit. Only then do you store 8 at index 0. At index 1 the value is 4 and the complement is 6. 6 is absent, so you store 4 at index 1. At index 2 the value is 5 and the complement is 5. 5 is not in the map yet, so this 5 does not pair with itself. You store 5 at index 2. At index 3 the value is 1 and the complement is 9. Miss, then store 1 at index 3. At index 4 the value is 7 and the complement is 3. Miss, then store 7 at index 4. At index 5 the value is 2 and the complement is 8. 8 is in the map at index 0. You return [0, 5].

Index 0 is a real index in that answer. A plain object would store the key as a string, and a truthiness check treats a stored 0 as missing. if (obj[need]) is false when the stored index is 0, so this same pair would be skipped. Map.has does not care that the index is 0.

The insert-after rule shows up on a one-element input. Take [3] with target 6. The complement is 3. The map is empty, so there is no hit. Then you insert 3 at index 0. You do not return [0, 0]. One 3 is not used twice. If you inserted before the lookup, the map would already contain 3, the lookup would hit, and you would pair the element with itself. Take [3, 3] with target 6 as the allowed version of a repeated value. Index 0 misses and stores 3 at 0. Index 1 looks up 3, hits index 0, and returns [0, 1]. Those are two different indexes.

[8, 4, 5, 1, 7, 2], target 10 i=0 need 2, miss, store 8 at 0 i=1 need 6, miss, store 4 at 1 i=2 need 5, miss, store 5 at 2 i=3 need 9, miss, store 1 at 3 i=4 need 3, miss, store 7 at 4 i=5 need 8, hit index 0, return [0, 5] [3], target 6: need 3, map empty, then store. Not [0, 0]. object bug: if (obj[need]) is false when the stored index is 0

How you recognise it

  • The array is not sorted, and the question at i is whether the complement target − x has already appeared.
  • You need a count of each value, the last index of a value, or the list of items that share one key.
  • You need the longest run of consecutive numbers, and the order of the array does not matter.
  • A repeated value should return true as soon as the set already contains it.
  • Two strings are anagrams when one frequency map built from the first string is consumed exactly by the second.
  • If the array is sorted and each comparison can throw away an end, a map is extra memory you do not need.

Use chapter 5 when the array is sorted and moving one end replaces the map.

Use chapter 3 when n is small enough that the nested loops are the whole answer.

Use chapter 8 when the key you look up is an earlier prefix sum, not the value itself.

What to say out loud

I look up the complement before I insert the current value, so a value is never paired with itself. I use a Map, because index 0 is a real index and an object check would treat it as missing. The map holds only indexes I have already finished.

The template

Map, not an object. Lookup, then insert.

function twoSum(nums, target) {
  const seen = new Map();
  for (let i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
    const need = target - nums[i];
    if (seen.has(need)) return [seen.get(need), i];
    seen.set(nums[i], i);
  }
  return [];
}

seen is a Map. The loop index starts at 0 and stops before nums.length, so every real index is visited and index n is not. need is the complement. seen.has(need) runs before seen.set. That order is the off-by-one that matters. Set first, and a value whose double is the target matches itself. The return gives the stored index and then i, which are two different positions. If the loop ends, there was no pair, and you return an empty array. A frequency update later in the chapter is freq.set(x, (freq.get(x) ?? 0) + 1). ?? replaces only undefined. A Set is the same idea when you store membership and nothing else.

The map can store more than the complement's index. An index of the complement is this template, inserted after the lookup. A last index answers "contains duplicate II": if this value was seen at j and i − j is at most k, return true, then store i. A frequency answers anagrams, good pairs, and intersections that must respect duplicate copies. Intersection II walks one array's counts down as you accept values from the other. A group stores a list under a shared key.

Groups that share a key

Take ["eat", "tea", "tan", "ate", "nat", "bat"]. The key of a word is its characters sorted and joined. Index 0 is "eat", and the key is "aet". The map has no list for "aet" yet, so you create one and push the original "eat". Index 1 is "tea". Its key is also "aet", so you push "tea" onto that same list. Index 2 is "tan", key "ant", a new list. Index 3 is "ate", key "aet", pushed onto the first list. Index 4 is "nat", key "ant". Index 5 is "bat", key "abt", a new list. The lists are ["eat", "tea", "ate"], ["tan", "nat"], and ["bat"]. A Map keeps insertion order, so the groups come out in the order of their first word.

The sorted spelling is the key. The original spelling is the value.

function groupAnagrams(strs) {
  const groups = new Map();
  for (const s of strs) {
    const key = s.split("").sort().join("");
    if (!groups.has(key)) groups.set(key, []);
    groups.get(key).push(s);
  }
  return [...groups.values()];
}

You build the key first. You create the list only when has says it is missing. You push s, the original spelling, after the list exists. split makes a new array of characters, so the sort does not rewrite s. If you sorted s itself and pushed that, the returned words would be the keys. The spread at the end copies the lists out of the map. An empty input returns an empty list of groups.

Longest consecutive run

Put every value in a set. Duplicates collapse, because a set stores each number once. A run should be counted once, from its start. The start is a value x whose predecessor x − 1 is absent. Take [100, 4, 200, 1, 3, 2]. 100 has no 99, so it starts. The next value 101 is absent, and the length is 1. Best becomes 1. 4 does not start, because 3 is in the set. You skip it. 200 has no 199, so it starts a run of length 1. Best stays 1. 1 has no 0, so it starts. You walk 1, then 2, then 3, then 4. The next value 5 is absent. The length is 4, and best becomes 4. 3 does not start, because 2 is present. 2 does not start, because 1 is present. The answer is 4.

If you started a walk at every number, the run 1, 2, 3, 4 would be walked from 1, from 2, from 3, and from 4. On a long run of length n the inner walks cost about n². Starting only when x − 1 is absent visits each number at most once inside the inner walk. The outer loop still looks at each number once. The total stays linear.

Only a number with no predecessor starts a run.

function longestConsecutive(nums) {
  const set = new Set(nums);
  let best = 0;
  for (const x of set) {
    if (set.has(x - 1)) continue;
    let y = x;
    while (set.has(y)) y += 1;
    if (y - x > best) best = y - x;
  }
  return best;
}

The set is built before the loop, so membership tests do not change it. The continue is the start rule, and it happens before y moves. The while loop steps y past the end of the run. The length is y - x, not y - x + 1, because y has already moved one past the last present value. An empty set never enters the loop, and best stays 0. The off-by-one people write is y - x + 1 after that loop, which reports one too many.

[100, 4, 200, 1, 3, 2] 100 starts a run of length 1 (99 is absent) 4 does not start (3 is present) 200 starts a run of length 1 1 starts: 1, 2, 3, 4 → length 4 3 and 2 do not start best = 4

Majority in one vote

Problem 169 promises that one value occurs more than n/2 times. You can count with a map. Boyer–Moore does it with two variables, a candidate and a vote count. Take [2, 2, 1, 1, 1, 2, 2]. Votes start at 0, so the first 2 becomes the candidate and the vote becomes 1. The next 2 raises the vote to 2. The next value is 1, and the vote falls to 1. The next 1 brings the vote to 0. The following 1 finds a vote of 0, so it becomes the candidate, and the vote becomes 1. The next value is 2, which cancels that vote back to 0. The last 2 finds a vote of 0, becomes the candidate, and leaves the vote at 1. The candidate is 2, which is the majority.

Pairs of "this candidate, some other value" cancel. The promised majority has more than half the seats, so it cannot be cancelled away. You do not need a second pass while the promise holds. If a follow-up removes the promise, you count the candidate afterward and check that count yourself.

169 promises a majority, so the candidate is the answer. No second pass.

function majorityElement(nums) {
  let candidate = 0;
  let votes = 0;
  for (const x of nums) {
    if (votes === 0) candidate = x;
    votes += x === candidate ? 1 : -1;
  }
  return candidate;
}

When votes is 0, you set the candidate to x before the vote changes. The same x then adds 1, because it now matches the candidate. If you added the vote first, a 0 vote would subtract 1 from a stale candidate and you would skip the assignment. The promise is why the return needs no count. Problem 169 does not ask for the count.

[2, 2, 1, 1, 1, 2, 2] 2 votes 1 2 votes 2 1 votes 1 1 votes 0 1 becomes candidate, votes 1 2 votes 0 2 becomes candidate, votes 1 answer 2

Every value that beats n/3

Problem 229 asks for every value that occurs more than n/3 times. There are at most two such values. The problem does not promise that a survivor is actually that frequent, so a second pass counts the survivors. Take [1, 1, 1, 3, 3, 2, 2, 2]. n is 8, and n/3 is about 2.67, so a value needs a count of at least 3. The first 1 takes candidate a, because a's count is 0. The next two 1s raise a's count to 3. The first 3 takes candidate b. The next 3 raises b's count to 2. The first 2 matches neither candidate, and both counts are nonzero, so both counts fall: a goes to 2 and b goes to 1. The next 2 cancels again: a goes to 1 and b goes to 0. The last 2 finds b's count at 0, so b becomes 2 and b's count becomes 1.

The second pass counts the real copies. a is 1 and occurs three times. b is 2 and occurs three times. Both beat n/3, so both are returned. The cancelled 3 does not survive as a candidate.

The second pass is the part you cannot skip. Take [1, 2, 3]. n is 3, so a count must be greater than 1. The 1 takes a, the 2 takes b, and the 3 cancels both counts to 0. The variables a and b still hold 1 and 2. A second pass finds one copy of each. One is not greater than 1, so the answer is empty. If you returned the survivors without counting, you would return values that are not frequent enough.

Two candidates. The second pass is required.

function majorityElement(nums) {
  let a = 0;
  let b = 0;
  let ca = 0;
  let cb = 0;
  for (const x of nums) {
    if (x === a) ca += 1;
    else if (x === b) cb += 1;
    else if (ca === 0) { a = x; ca = 1; }
    else if (cb === 0) { b = x; cb = 1; }
    else { ca -= 1; cb -= 1; }
  }
  ca = 0;
  cb = 0;
  for (const x of nums) {
    if (x === a) ca += 1;
    else if (x === b) cb += 1;
  }
  const out = [];
  if (ca > nums.length / 3) out.push(a);
  if (cb > nums.length / 3) out.push(b);
  return out;
}

The first loop tests equality with a, then equality with b, then an empty count, and only then a cancel. That order matters. If you tested an empty count before equality, a live candidate could be overwritten by a value you should have counted. After the first loop you set both counts back to 0 before the second walk. The second walk is a real count, not another vote. You push a value only when its real count is greater than n/3. The division is numeric, so you compare with nums.length / 3 and you do not round the threshold down by hand.

Top K frequent values can sit in buckets. The bucket index is the frequency, and the bucket holds the values with that frequency. Walk the buckets from the high end until you have k values. A heap also works. The bucket version is linear after the frequency map. 4Sum II splits four arrays into two pairs. Map every sum of a pair from the first two arrays, then ask the map for the negation of each pair from the last two. You add the stored count, because many pairs can share a sum.

Time and extra memory

A pass of expected constant-time lookups is expected O(n) time and O(n) extra memory for the map or the set. The promised majority vote is O(n) time and O(1) extra memory. Majority II adds a second count, still O(n) time and O(1) extra memory.

Mistakes

  • Inserting before the lookup on [3] with target 6 returns a pair that uses 3 twice. The map already holds 3 when you ask for 3, so you return [0, 0]. Lookup first. The map is empty on that single element.
  • Writing if (seen[need]) on an object fails when the stored index is 0. On [8, 4, 5, 1, 7, 2] with target 10, the hit is index 0. The object check is false, and you miss [0, 5]. Use Map.has.
  • Starting a consecutive run at every number still finds 4 on [100, 4, 200, 1, 3, 2], and the time becomes quadratic on a long run such as [1, 2, 3, ..., n]. Start only when x − 1 is absent.
  • Skipping the second pass on majority II fails on [1, 2, 3]. The cancelled candidates still sit in the variables, and returning them reports values whose counts are not above n/3. The second pass returns an empty list.
  • Using the vote variable as a count for problem 169 is a different question. 169 does not ask for the count. If a follow-up removes the promise, count the candidate in a second pass before you trust it.

Practice

Stay before chapter 5. Then before you leave the chapter. Majority II is a second pass.

  1. 1. Two Sum easy Stay

    Lookup the complement, then insert. Exactly one answer exists. You may not use the same element twice.

  2. 217. Contains Duplicate easy Stay

    A set of values seen so far. A repeat returns true.

  3. 242. Valid Anagram easy Stay

    One frequency map built from s and consumed by t, or two maps compared. Lengths must match.

  4. 128. Longest Consecutive Sequence medium Stay

    Start a run only when x − 1 is absent. Duplicates disappear inside the set.

  5. 169. Majority Element easy Stay

    The vote. The promise means you return the candidate.

  6. 219. Contains Duplicate II easy Then

    Store the last index. The test is i − j <= k.

  7. 349. Intersection of Two Arrays easy Then

    A set of the first array. Emit a value from the second only once.

  8. 350. Intersection of Two Arrays II easy Then

    Frequencies. Each accepted copy consumes one count.

  9. 1207. Unique Number of Occurrences easy Then

    Map value to count, then a set of those counts. The set must be the same size as the map.

  10. 2215. Find the Difference of Two Arrays easy Then

    Two sets. A value belongs to the first answer list when it is missing from the second set.

  11. 49. Group Anagrams medium Then

    Key by the sorted characters. The original strings stay intact inside the lists.

  12. 347. Top K Frequent Elements medium Then

    Frequency map, then buckets by count, then walk from the highest count.

  13. 454. 4Sum II medium Then

    Map sums of A[i] + B[j]. For each C[k] + D[l], add the count of the negation.

  14. 229. Majority Element II medium Second pass

    At most two candidates, then a real count. Keep a value only when count > n/3.

Rewrite from memory

Rewrite two sum and the consecutive-run start rule from a blank file, with the lookup before the insert.

Carry forward. Chapter 5 uses two indexes on a sorted array, so the complement search no longer needs a map.

Chapter 05 · builds on chapters 3 and 4

Two pointers from the ends

On a sorted array, or on a palindrome, each step can throw away one end. A pointer here is an index you store in a variable.

What this pattern is

A pointer, the first time this chapter uses the word, is an index stored in an integer. It is not a memory address. Two pointers from the ends means two indexes. l starts at 0, the first index. r starts at the last index, n − 1. You are on a sorted array, or you are comparing the two ends of a string. Each step throws away one end forever.

The unchecked pairs are the ones that still sit between l and r. An invariant is a sentence that stays true every time you are about to move. Here it is: every pair you have not checked still has both ends inside that range, and the move you are about to make throws away only pairs that cannot be the answer. Picture a sorted row of cards on a table. Your left hand holds the smallest remaining card. Your right hand holds the largest. If the sum is too small, the left card can never work with anything still on the table, so the left hand steps in.

What you already know

Chapter 3 could test every pair with two loops. Chapter 4 could find a complement with a map. A complement is the other value that finishes the pair. If the array is sorted, the complement has a direction, and you can drop the map. This chapter adds the two ends and the rule for which end moves.

A worked example

Take the sorted array [1, 2, 3, 5, 8, 11] and target 10. There are six values. l starts at index 0, value 1. r starts at index 5, value 11. The sum is 12, which is too large. The right value is too big even when it is paired with the smallest remaining partner, so you move r to 4. Now the right value is 8. The sum is 1 + 8 = 9, which is too small. The left value is too small even when it is paired with the largest remaining partner, so you move l to 1. The left value is 2 and the right value is still 8. The sum is 10. You stop.

The problem wants 1-based indexes, so you return [l + 1, r + 1], which is [2, 5]. Returning [1, 4] would be the 0-based pair, and it is the wrong answer for this problem. You never let l and r land on the same index. One index is not a pair. If the sum had stayed wrong until the ends met, you would return an empty array. This problem promises exactly one solution, so the equal-sum line does hit.

[1, 2, 3, 5, 8, 11], target 10 l=0 r=5 sum 12, too big, r = 4 l=0 r=4 sum 9, too small, l = 1 l=1 r=4 sum 10, return [2, 5]

How you recognise it

  • The array is sorted, or you are allowed to sort it and you do not need the original indexes afterward.
  • You compare the two ends: a sum against a target, a container's walls, the larger square, or a pair of characters.
  • Each step can throw away one end forever, because every pair that used that end is impossible or useless.
  • A sum that is too small can only be repaired by moving the left index right, on a non-decreasing array.
  • A sum that is too large can only be repaired by moving the right index left.
  • If both indexes are only allowed to move right, you are in a later chapter.
