Use chapter 4 when the values are not sorted and you still need the complement in a map.

Use chapter 6 when both indexes move right and one of them writes into the array.

Use chapter 7 when both indexes move right to bound one contiguous band, and you are not throwing an end away forever.

What to say out loud

Every unchecked pair is still between l and r. If the sum is too small I move l right, because this left value cannot reach the target with any remaining partner. If the sum is too large I move r left. I stop when l and r meet, because one index is not a pair.

The template

Too small moves l. Too large moves r. This problem's answer is 1-based.

function twoSum(numbers, target) {
  let l = 0;
  let r = numbers.length - 1;
  while (l < r) {
    const sum = numbers[l] + numbers[r];
    if (sum === target) return [l + 1, r + 1];
    if (sum < target) l += 1;
    else r -= 1;
  }
  return [];
}

The invariant is the live range: any pair that could still hit the target uses an index from l through r. l starts at 0 and r starts at the last index. The loop condition is l < r. When they are equal you would read one cell twice. You compute the sum before you move. Equality returns [l + 1, r + 1]. The added 1 is this problem's 1-based answer, and it is the off-by-one people drop. A sum below the target increments l only. Any other sum decrements r only. You do not move both ends on a miss. Moving both would skip the pair that uses the new left value with the old right value.

The container

Take heights [1, 8, 6, 2, 5, 4, 8, 3, 7]. There are nine lines, indexed 0 through 8. Width is the distance between the indexes. Height is the shorter wall. l starts at 0, height 1. r starts at 8, height 7. The area is 1 × 8 = 8. The left wall is shorter, so you move l to 1. Now the walls are 8 and 7. The width is 7. The area is 7 × 7 = 49, and best becomes 49. The right wall is shorter, so you move r to 7. The new right height is 3. The area is 3 × 6 = 18, which does not beat 49. The right wall is still shorter, so r moves to 6. The walls are 8 and 8. The area is 8 × 5 = 40. The walls are equal, so the code moves r, because the left wall is not strictly shorter. You keep going. Later areas are 16, 15, 4, and 6. None of them beats 49. The answer is 49.

Width only shrinks as the ends move in. Suppose the left wall is the shorter one. You have just measured this pair. Every other pairing that still uses this same left wall uses some index strictly between the two ends. That inner index is closer, so the width is smaller. The height of that inner pairing is the minimum of the left wall and the inner height. A minimum is at most the left wall, so the height is at most this left wall. Every inner pairing of this left wall has smaller width and height at most that wall. None of those inner pairs can beat the area you just measured. You move the left index, and you never use that short wall again.

If the right wall is shorter, the same argument says every inner pairing of that right wall is worse, and you move r left. If the walls are equal, that shared height still cannot get better while the width shrinks, so moving either end is safe. The code moves the right end in the equal case, because it tests whether the left wall is strictly shorter. If you moved the taller wall at the start of this array, you would leave the short left wall in place and skip the pair of index 1 and index 8. That pair holds area 49, and you would not get it back.

Move the shorter wall only.

function maxArea(height) {
  let l = 0;
  let r = height.length - 1;
  let best = 0;
  while (l < r) {
    const h = Math.min(height[l], height[r]);
    const area = h * (r - l);
    if (area > best) best = area;
    if (height[l] < height[r]) l += 1;
    else r -= 1;
  }
  return best;
}

You measure the area before you move. h is the shorter wall, and the width is r - l, not r - l + 1. The slots between the walls are the water, and the distance between indexes is the width. After the measurement, you move only the shorter side. The else covers a shorter right wall and an equal pair, and in both of those cases r decreases. You do not move both ends in one step. The loop stops when l and r meet, because a container needs two distinct indexes.

[1, 8, 6, 2, 5, 4, 8, 3, 7] l=0 (1), r=8 (7), area 8, left is shorter, l = 1 l=1 (8), r=8 (7), area 49, right is shorter, r = 7 keep going. 49 is the best. moving the taller wall first would skip this pair and never recover it.

Squares, written from the back

A sorted array of negatives and positives has its largest squares at the ends, because squaring turns a large negative into a large positive. Take [-4, -1, 0, 3, 10]. There are five values. The output has five slots, and the write index starts at 4, the last slot. l is 0 and holds −4. r is 4 and holds 10. The absolute values are 4 and 10. 10 is larger, so you write 100 at index 4 and move r to 3. Now the ends are −4 and 3. Absolute values 4 and 3. 4 is larger, so you write 16 at index 3 and move l to 1. The ends are −1 and 3. 3 is larger, so you write 9 at index 2 and move r to 2. The ends are −1 and 0. 1 is larger, so you write 1 at index 1 and move l to 2. Both ends now sit on 0. You write 0 at index 0. The output is [0, 1, 9, 16, 100].

The next output cell is the larger of the two end squares.

function sortedSquares(nums) {
  const n = nums.length;
  const out = new Array(n);
  let l = 0;
  let r = n - 1;
  for (let i = n - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
    if (Math.abs(nums[l]) > Math.abs(nums[r])) {
      out[i] = nums[l] * nums[l];
      l += 1;
    } else {
      out[i] = nums[r] * nums[r];
      r -= 1;
    }
  }
  return out;
}

You compare the absolute values before you write. The larger end is squared into out[i], and only that end moves. i walks from n − 1 down to 0, so the largest square lands at the end and the smallest square lands at index 0. The off-by-one is the write direction. If you wrote from the front, you would not yet know the smallest square, and the first cell would get a value that belongs at the end. When the absolute values tie, the else takes the right end. Either end is a correct square in a tie. An empty input builds an empty output and the loop does not run.

[-4, -1, 0, 3, 10] write 100 from the right, then 16 from the left, then 9, then 1, then 0 [0, 1, 9, 16, 100]

A palindrome is the same two indexes

Take the string "a,c@a". The indexes are 0 a, 1 ,, 2 c, 3 @, 4 a. l starts at 0 and r starts at 4. You lowercase both ends. Both are the letter a, so they match, and both ends step inward. Now l is 1 and r is 3. The left character is a comma. It is not a letter or a digit, so you move only l, to 2, and you do not touch r on that turn. Now the left character is c and the right character is @. @ is not a letter or a digit, so you move only r, to 2. l and r now sit on the same index, and the loop stops. One middle character does not need a partner. The string is a palindrome after the skips.

A mismatch returns immediately. On "ab@ca" the outer a characters match, then b and c are both real characters and they differ, so you return false before any further skip. You do not repair a mismatch by moving only one end in this problem.

Skip non-letters with a named helper.

function isAlnum(ch) {
  return (ch >= "a" && ch <= "z") || (ch >= "0" && ch <= "9");
}

function isPalindrome(s) {
  let l = 0;
  let r = s.length - 1;
  while (l < r) {
    const L = s[l].toLowerCase();
    const R = s[r].toLowerCase();
    if (!isAlnum(L)) { l += 1; continue; }
    if (!isAlnum(R)) { r -= 1; continue; }
    if (L !== R) return false;
    l += 1;
    r -= 1;
  }
  return true;
}

You lowercase both characters before any test, so A and a can match. The helper answers "is this a letter or a digit?" If the left character fails, you increment l and continue, which skips the right-hand test on that turn. If the right character fails, you decrement r and continue. Only two real characters reach the inequality test. A difference returns false at once. A match moves both ends. The loop condition l < r is the off-by-one. Letting them cross, or comparing when they are equal, treats one index as a pair. An empty string never enters the loop and returns true.

Backspace, still from the ends

You can compare two strings with backspaces without building the edited text. A pointer here is still an index. You walk backward. Take s = "ab#c" and t = "ad#c". Both end at index 3, the character c. Neither end is a #, so both real characters are c, they match, and both indexes step to 2. From index 2, s sees #. That raises a skip count to 1 and moves to index 1. Index 1 is b, and a skip is owed, so the skip is spent and the index moves to 0. Index 0 is a with no skip owed, so a is the next real character. The same walk on t spends the # on d and also lands on a. The characters match. Both indexes then step to −1. The next search from −1 finds nothing on either string, so both sides are exhausted together and the strings match.

Each call returns the next real character at or before i.

function nextIndex(s, i) {
  let skip = 0;
  while (i >= 0) {
    if (s[i] === "#") {
      skip += 1;
      i -= 1;
    } else if (skip > 0) {
      skip -= 1;
      i -= 1;
    } else {
      return i;
    }
  }
  return -1;
}

function backspaceCompare(s, t) {
  let i = s.length - 1;
  let j = t.length - 1;
  while (i >= 0 || j >= 0) {
    i = nextIndex(s, i);
    j = nextIndex(t, j);
    if (i < 0 && j < 0) return true;
    if (i < 0 || j < 0 || s[i] !== t[j]) return false;
    i -= 1;
    j -= 1;
  }
  return true;
}

nextIndex starts at the index you hand it and walks left. A # adds 1 to skip and moves left. A real character with skip > 0 spends one skip and moves left. It is not returned. A real character with skip 0 is returned, and it is not consumed inside the helper. The caller compares those two indexes, and only after a match does the caller step one past each of them. If you stepped inside the helper before returning, you would skip the character you meant to compare. If one side returns −1 and the other still has a character, the strings differ. "a#c" against "c" matches. "ab#c" against "c" does not, because the a is still there after the # deletes b.

Time and extra memory

Each end moves inward at most n times, so these templates are O(n) time and O(1) extra memory after the array is sorted. Squares allocates the output array of length n, which the problem requires.

Mistakes

  • Moving both ends because the area felt small fails on [1, 8, 6, 2, 5, 4, 8, 3, 7]. The first area is only 8, and the left wall is the short one. If you move the taller right wall, you skip the later pair that holds area 49, and that pair does not come back.
  • Returning the 0-based pair for two sum II fails on [1, 2, 3, 5, 8, 11] with target 10. The indexes are 1 and 4. The required answer is [2, 5]. The code has to add 1 to each index.
  • Writing squares from the front fails on [-4, -1, 0, 3, 10]. The largest square is 100, at an end, so the first output cell is not known until the small middle squares are left. The write index has to start at the last slot.
  • Letting l and r cross, or using l <= r as a pair loop, treats one index as a pair. On a single-character string the two indexes start equal. There is no pair to compare, and the palindrome is true.
  • Replacing the palindrome filter with a chain of replaces you cannot read out loud hides which character moved. The named helper is the version you can walk on "a,c@a".

Practice

Stay before chapter 6. Then before you leave the chapter.

  1. 167. Two Sum II - Input Array Is Sorted medium Stay

    Sorted, exactly one solution, answer is 1-based. Too small moves l.

  2. 11. Container With Most Water medium Stay

    Move the shorter side only. Width is r − l, height is the min of the two walls.

  3. 977. Squares of a Sorted Array easy Stay

    Write from index n − 1. Compare absolute values at the ends.

  4. 125. Valid Palindrome easy Stay

    Skip non-alphanumeric characters. Compare lowercase letters and digits.

  5. 344. Reverse String easy Then

    Swap s[l] and s[r], then move both ends in. The input is a character array.

  6. 345. Reverse Vowels of a String easy Then

    Same swap, but an end moves alone until it sits on a vowel.

  7. 680. Valid Palindrome II easy Then

    On the first mismatch, test the slice that skips l, and the slice that skips r.

  8. 844. Backspace String Compare easy Then

    The skip count from the right. A stack of kept characters is the version you can write if you blank on the skip.

Rewrite from memory

Rewrite two sum II and the container loop from a blank file, and say why a shorter left wall cannot win with any index inside it.

Carry forward. Chapter 6 keeps two indexes, and both of them move to the right. One reads. One writes.

Chapter 06 · builds on chapter 5

Same-direction two pointers

One index reads every cell. The other index writes, and it moves only when the cell is kept.

What this pattern is

A pointer is an index stored in an integer. It is not a memory address. This chapter uses two pointers that move in the same direction, toward the right. r reads every cell. w writes, and w advances only when the cell you just read is a cell you are keeping. In place means you overwrite the input with a handful of extra variables, not a second array of length n. The kept prefix is nums[0..w). A prefix is the block from the start up to, but not including, index w. After the loop, w is the length the caller should read.

An invariant is a sentence that stays true when you finish a read. Here it is: r has consumed every index before the current read, and nums[0..w) is exactly the kept version of that consumed prefix. Picture a clerk copying a form onto the same page. The reading finger visits every line. The writing finger moves only when the line is one you keep. The gap between the fingers is the lines you have decided to drop.

What you already know

Chapter 5 moved two indexes toward each other and threw one end away. Both indexes in this chapter move right, and one of them writes. You already know an in-place answer returns a length and leaves the tail as garbage. This chapter adds the read index, the write index, and the rule for when the write index steps.

A worked example

Take [3, 2, 2, 3, 4, 3] and remove every 3. There are six values. w starts at 0, so the kept prefix is empty. r starts at 0. Index 0 holds 3, which is the value you are removing, so you do not write and w stays 0. Index 1 holds 2. You write 2 at index 0, then w becomes 1. The kept prefix is [2]. Index 2 holds 2. You write 2 at index 1, then w becomes 2. The prefix is [2, 2]. Index 3 holds 3, so you skip it. Index 4 holds 4. You write 4 at index 2, then w becomes 3. The prefix is [2, 2, 4]. Index 5 holds 3, so you skip it. You return 3. The caller reads the first 3 slots. The tail may still show old numbers, and the caller ignores it.

The write happens before w grows. If you grew w first, you would skip a slot and the kept values would shift right. If you grew w on a 3, the removed value would sit inside the prefix. On this input that bug returns a length that still counts a 3.

[3, 2, 2, 3, 4, 3], remove 3 r=0 skip r=1 write 2 at 0, w = 1 r=2 write 2 at 1, w = 2 r=3 skip r=4 write 4 at 2, w = 3 r=5 skip return 3, prefix [2, 2, 4]

How you recognise it

  • The array is sorted and you overwrite duplicates in place, returning the new length.
  • You filter values out, or you slide zeros to the end, and the relative order of the kept values has to stay.
  • You merge two sorted arrays into one buffer that already has empty room at the end.
  • The read index visits every cell, and the write index moves only for a cell that stays.
  • You check whether one sequence appears inside another in order. That is one index per sequence, and both move forward.
  • If the two indexes move toward each other, or if you must not overwrite the array, this is a different chapter.

Use chapter 5 when the two indexes move toward each other on a sorted array or a palindrome.

Use chapter 7 when both indexes move right to mark a window, and the array itself stays unchanged.

Use chapter 13 when each value should be moved onto the index that matches the value.

What to say out loud

r reads every cell from left to right. w writes, and w moves only when I keep the cell I just read. When I finish a read, nums from 0 up to but not including w is the kept copy of what r has consumed. The length I return is w, not the last kept index.

The template

w advances only when the value stays.

function removeElement(nums, val) {
  let w = 0;
  for (let r = 0; r < nums.length; r++) {
    if (nums[r] !== val) {
      nums[w] = nums[r];
      w += 1;
    }
  }
  return w;
}

The invariant is the kept prefix nums[0..w) for every index r has already passed. w and r both start at 0. The loop runs r through every index, including the last one, and stops before nums.length. Inside the true branch you assign nums[w] = nums[r] first, then you add 1 to w. That order fills the current hole and then moves the hole forward. Adding first would leave a stale slot at the old w and write past the kept prefix. You do not add to w when the value equals val. The returned number is w, which is a length. On an empty array the loop never runs and the length is 0. On an array with no copies of val, w ends equal to n, and every write lands on the same index it read.

Move zeroes is this write cursor on the non-zeros, followed by a fill. Take [0, 1, 0, 3, 12]. Skip the 0 at index 0. Write 1 at index 0 and advance. Skip the next 0. Write 3, then write 12. The write index is now 3, and the array looks like [1, 3, 12, 3, 12] before the fill. You then set every slot from 3 to the end to 0. The result is [1, 3, 12, 0, 0]. The non-zeros kept their order. A blind swap that pulls a later zero backward can reorder those non-zeros. Write the keepers forward, then set the tail.

Duplicates in a sorted array

Sorted duplicates sit next to each other. Take [1, 1, 2, 2, 3] and keep one copy of each value. The array is not empty, so index 0 is kept without a comparison, and w starts at 1. r starts at 1. Index 1 holds 1, and the last kept value is nums[0], which is also 1, so you skip. Index 2 holds 2, which differs from 1. You write 2 at index 1, then w becomes 2. The array begins [1, 2, ...]. Index 3 holds 2, and the last kept value is now nums[1], which is 2, so you skip. Index 4 holds 3, which differs from 2. You write 3 at index 2, then w becomes 3. You return 3. The caller reads [1, 2, 3].

A one-element array never enters the loop. w stays 1, and that length is correct. An empty array returns 0 before w is set, so you never read index −1.

Allowing two copies uses a different comparison. Take [1, 1, 1, 2, 2, 3]. w starts at 0, not at 1. The test w < 2 lets the first two writes happen with no look backward. Index 0 writes 1, and w becomes 1. Index 1 writes 1, and w becomes 2. Index 2 holds 1, and nums[w - 2] is nums[0], which is 1, so this third 1 is skipped. Index 3 holds 2, and nums[0] is 1, so you write 2 at index 2 and w becomes 3. Index 4 holds 2. nums[w - 2] is now nums[1], which is 1, not 2, so you write another 2 and w becomes 4. Index 5 holds 3, which differs from nums[2], so you write 3 and w becomes 5. The kept prefix is [1, 1, 2, 2, 3].

One copy compares with w − 1. Two copies compare with w − 2.

function removeDuplicates(nums) {
  if (nums.length === 0) return 0;
  let w = 1;
  for (let r = 1; r < nums.length; r++) {
    if (nums[r] !== nums[w - 1]) {
      nums[w] = nums[r];
      w += 1;
    }
  }
  return w;
}

function removeDuplicatesII(nums) {
  let w = 0;
  for (let r = 0; r < nums.length; r++) {
    if (w < 2 || nums[r] !== nums[w - 2]) {
      nums[w] = nums[r];
      w += 1;
    }
  }
  return w;
}

For one copy, the empty check comes first. Then w starts at 1 so the comparison nums[w - 1] is index 0, never −1. You compare, then write at w, then increment. Comparing with nums[r - 1] also works for one copy on a sorted array, because duplicates are adjacent and you are keeping the first of each run. For two copies that neighbor test is the wrong slot. On [1, 1, 1] a test against nums[r - 1] sees equal neighbors and keeps only the first 1. The allowed prefix is [1, 1], length 2. You look at the kept prefix, at w - 2, and the w < 2 guard lets the first two keepers through before that index exists. The third 1 equals nums[0] and is dropped.

[1, 1, 1, 2, 2, 3], at most two copies r=0 write, w=1 r=1 write, w=2 r=2 value 1 equals nums[0], skip r=3 value 2, write, w=3 r=4 value 2, nums[w-2] is 1, write, w=4 r=5 value 3, write, w=5 prefix [1, 1, 2, 2, 3]

Merge from the end

Take nums1 = [1, 2, 3, 0, 0, 0] with m = 3, and nums2 = [2, 5, 6] with n = 3. The empty slots are the tail. The write index starts at m + n − 1, which is 5. i starts at 2, on the 3 in nums1. j starts at 2, on the 6 in nums2. 3 is not greater than 6, so you write 6 at index 5 and move j to 1. Next, 3 is not greater than 5, so you write 5 at index 4 and move j to 0. Next, 3 is greater than 2, so you write 3 at index 3 and move i to 1. Next, nums1[1] is 2 and nums2[0] is 2. 2 is not greater than 2, so the else writes the 2 from nums2 at index 2 and j becomes −1. The loop stops because nums2 is exhausted. Index 0 still holds 1 and index 1 still holds 2. They were already in the right place. The merged array is [1, 2, 2, 3, 5, 6].

The empty tail is the space you write into.

function merge(nums1, m, nums2, n) {
  let i = m - 1;
  let j = n - 1;
  let w = m + n - 1;
  while (j >= 0) {
    if (i >= 0 && nums1[i] > nums2[j]) {
      nums1[w] = nums1[i];
      i -= 1;
    } else {
      nums1[w] = nums2[j];
      j -= 1;
    }
    w -= 1;
  }
}

You choose the value before you move w. The test reads nums1[i] only when i is still at least 0. If i has already fallen off, every remaining write copies nums2. When the two tails are equal, the test fails and you take nums2. That choice is safe, because the equal value in nums1 is still sitting in the unprocessed prefix. The loop condition is j >= 0, not w >= 0. When nums2 runs out, the leftover nums1 prefix is already home, so you stop. Writing from index 0 would overwrite a nums1 value you have not merged. On this input the first front write would destroy the 1.

nums1 = [1, 2, 3, 0, 0, 0], m = 3 nums2 = [2, 5, 6], n = 3 write 6, then 5, then 3, then 2 from nums2 j is exhausted, so the leftover [1, 2] stays where it is [1, 2, 2, 3, 5, 6]

Sort by parity uses the same write slot as a swap destination. Each even number is swapped into the next write index, so the evens occupy a prefix and the odds fall behind. Sort by parity II keeps two cursors, one on even indexes and one on odd indexes. An even index wants an even value. When both cursors are sitting on the wrong kind of value, you swap them. On [4, 2, 5, 7] index 1 holds 2 and index 2 holds 5. Both are wrong. One swap produces [4, 5, 2, 7].

Is subsequence is the forward pair on two strings, and it does not overwrite anything. i walks the pattern. j walks the text. On a match you advance i. You always advance j. The pattern is a subsequence when i reaches the pattern length. A subsequence keeps order and allows gaps. Take pattern "abc" and text "ahbgdc". Index 0 of the text matches a, so the pattern index moves to 1. h does not match b. b does, so the pattern index moves to 2. g and d do not match c. c does, and the pattern index reaches 3. The answer is true. If the text ended before i reached the end, the answer would be false.

Time and extra memory

Remove and the duplicate walks are O(n) time, and the merge is O(m + n) time. Extra memory is O(1) besides the buffer the problem already gave you.

Mistakes

  • Advancing w on a value you meant to delete fails on [3, 2, 2, 3] when you remove 3. The first 3 is copied into the kept prefix, and the returned length counts a value that should have been dropped.
  • Comparing two-copy duplicates with nums[r − 1] only fails on [1, 1, 1]. Equal neighbors make you keep a single 1. The allowed prefix is two 1s. The comparison has to look at nums[w − 2] in the kept prefix, after the first two writes have been allowed through.
  • Merging from index 0 fails on nums1 = [1, 2, 3, 0, 0, 0] and nums2 = [2, 5, 6]. The first write lands on the 1, and that 1 is gone before it can be placed. The empty slots are at the end, so the write cursor starts at the end.
  • Filling moved zeros by swapping blindly can pull a zero between non-zeros and change their order. On [0, 1, 0, 3, 12] the keepers must stay [1, 3, 12] before the tail becomes zeros.

Practice

Stay before chapter 7. Then before you leave the chapter.

  1. 27. Remove Element easy Stay

    Write every value that is not val. Return the new length.

  2. 26. Remove Duplicates from Sorted Array easy Stay

    Sorted. Keep a value when it differs from the last kept value.

  3. 283. Move Zeroes easy Stay

    Pack the non-zeros, then fill zeros. Relative order stays.

  4. 88. Merge Sorted Array easy Stay

    Write from m + n − 1. Stop when nums2 is exhausted.

  5. 80. Remove Duplicates from Sorted Array II medium Then

    Keep the value when w < 2 or it differs from nums[w − 2].

  6. 905. Sort Array By Parity easy Then

    Swap evens into the next write slot. Odds fall behind.

  7. 922. Sort Array By Parity II easy Then

    Even indices want evens. Walk an even cursor and an odd cursor, and swap when both are wrong.

  8. 392. Is Subsequence easy Then

    Advance the pattern index only on a match. The text index always advances.

Rewrite from memory

Rewrite remove-duplicates and the merge-from-the-end loop from a blank file, including why the write cursor starts at the last slot.

Carry forward. Chapter 7 uses two indexes that both move right, and now they bound a window instead of overwriting the array.

Chapter 07 · builds on chapter 6

Sliding window

A window is the band from index l through index r. You grow it from the right, and you shrink it from the left with a while loop, when a bigger band only gets worse.

What this pattern is

A window is the band of cells from index l through index r. Both ends are included. A subarray is that kind of band: a stretch of cells with no gaps.

Picture a short ruler laid on a row of numbers. You step the right end forward one cell. When the cells under the ruler break the rule, you pull the left end forward until the rule holds again. You do not move either end backward.

This is worth doing when the test is monotone. Monotone means a bigger band only moves you toward illegal, and dropping cells from the left moves you back toward legal. Sums of values that are never negative work that way. Adding a value cannot make the sum smaller. Dropping the left value cannot make the sum larger.

You shrink with a while, not with a single if. One step of l can leave the window illegal, so you keep moving l until the rule holds.

What you already know

Chapter 6 already walks two indices that both move right. There r reads every cell, and the write index moves only when you keep a cell. A window keeps those two indices and gives them a new job: they are the two ends of one subarray. Chapter 5 started at the two ends of the whole array and moved inward. Here both ends begin on the left, and the right end leads.

A worked example

Use the array [2, 3, 1, 2, 4, 3] and the target 7. l starts at 0 and the sum starts at 0. r is the next index you include.

At r = 0 you add 2. The sum is 2, which is below 7, so l stays at 0. Dropping the only cell would make the sum smaller, and you still need to reach 7.

At r = 1 you add 3. The sum is 5. It is still below 7, so you leave l at 0 and let the band grow. At r = 2 you add 1. The sum is 6. A shrink cannot help here, because every value is at least 0, so a shorter band has a smaller sum.

At r = 3 you add 2. The window is indexes 0 through 3, the cells 2, 3, 1, 2, and the sum is 8. The length is r − l + 1, which is 4. The sum is at least 7, so you record 4. You then shrink, because a shorter legal window would be a better answer. You subtract nums[0], the sum falls to 6, and l moves to 1. The sum is below 7, so the while stops.

At r = 4 you add 4. The window is indexes 1 through 4, the sum is 10, and the length is 4. You subtract nums[1], which is 3. The sum is 7 and l moves to 2. The sum is still at least 7, so the while does not stop. The length is 4 − 2 + 1, which is 3, and you record 3. You subtract nums[2], which is 1. The sum is 6 and l moves to 3. Now the while stops. One if would have stopped when the sum was still 7, and you would never have recorded 3.

At r = 5 you add 3. The window is indexes 3 through 5, the cells 2, 4, 3, and the sum is 9. You subtract nums[3], the sum is 7, and l moves to 4. The length is 5 − 4 + 1, which is 2, so you record 2. You subtract nums[4], which is 4. The sum is 3 and l moves to 5. The while stops. The best length is 2. That window is the subarray [4, 3].

The same steps, packed into a trace, look like this.

[2, 3, 1, 2, 4, 3], target 7 r=3, window [2, 3, 1, 2], sum 8, length 4 drop 2, sum 6, stop. best = 4 r=4, add 4, window [3, 1, 2, 4], sum 10, length 4 drop 3, sum 7, length 3 drop 1, sum 6, stop. best = 3 r=5, add 3, window [2, 4, 3], sum 9, length 3 drop 2, sum 7, length 2 drop 4, sum 3, stop. best = 2 answer 2

How you recognise it

  • The cells you care about form a subarray, so there are no gaps in the band.
  • The test uses a sum, a product, a count of zeros, or a count of distinct values.
  • Making the band longer only pushes the test toward illegal, and shortening it from the left pushes the test back.
  • The values that the test uses are non-negative, or they are counts you can undo when a cell leaves the left edge.
  • You want a shortest band, a longest band, or a count of the bands that end at the r you just added.

Some prompts look close and belong in another chapter.

  • Use chapter 8 when a value can be negative and you need every subarray whose sum equals k, because a negative can make a later sum smaller.
  • Use chapter 5 when the array is sorted and each step can drop one end of the whole array.
  • Use chapter 15 when you need the maximum inside the window after that maximum slides out the left edge. One variable does not remember the second maximum.

What to say out loud

An invariant is a fact that is still true after every move. Yours is this: r is the end you just included, and l is the leftmost start that keeps [l, r] valid.

You move l with a while, because one step may not restore the invariant. The length you record is r − l + 1, and you record it before you drop nums[l].

The template

Non-negative values. Record the length before you shrink.

function minSubArrayLen(target, nums) {
  let l = 0;
  let sum = 0;
  let best = Infinity;
  for (let r = 0; r < nums.length; r++) {
    sum += nums[r];
    while (sum >= target) {
      const len = r - l + 1;
      if (len < best) best = len;
      sum -= nums[l];
      l += 1;
    }
  }
  return best === Infinity ? 0 : best;
}

After the while, the sum of nums[l..r] is below the target, or l has moved past r. You add nums[r] before you test the loop. Inside the loop you read the length, then you subtract nums[l], then you add 1 to l.

The length is r − l + 1. You must read it before the subtract. If you subtract first, you record a window that no longer meets the target. The test is a while because one step of l can leave the sum still at least the target. On the step where r is 4, the sum goes from 10 to 7, and 7 still qualifies, so a second shrink has to run at that same r.

Fixed length

A window of length k does not hunt for l. Use [1, 12, -5, -6, 50, 3] and k = 4. The first window is indexes 0 through 3. Its sum is 1 + 12 + (-5) + (-6), which is 2. You store 2 as the best sum.

Then r moves to 4. The new cell is 50. The cell that falls out is at index r − k, which is 0, and that cell is 1. The sum becomes 2 + 50 − 1, which is 51. You store 51. Then r moves to 5. You add 3 and you subtract the cell at index 1, which is 12. The sum becomes 42. The best sum stays 51, and the average is 51 / 4, which is 12.75.

Subtract the cell that just left the window.

function findMaxAverage(nums, k) {
  let sum = 0;
  for (let i = 0; i < k; i++) sum += nums[i];
  let best = sum;
  for (let r = k; r < nums.length; r++) {
    sum += nums[r];
    sum -= nums[r - k];
    if (sum > best) best = sum;
  }
  return best / k;
}
[1, 12, -5, -6, 50, 3], k = 4 first window sum = 1 + 12 + (-5) + (-6) = 2 next: drop 1, add 50, sum = 51 next: drop 12, add 3, sum = 42 best sum 51, average 12.75

l is not a free index here. The window always holds k cells, so the cell that leaves is at r − k. You add nums[r] and then you subtract nums[r − k]. If you subtract first, you remove a cell that is still inside the old window. The opening loop fills the first k cells before any cell leaves, and best starts as that first sum, so an array whose length is k still has an answer.

One deletion is required

Max consecutive ones III counts zeros in the window and shrinks while that count is above k. A flip keeps the cell, so the answer is the ordinary length r − l + 1. On [1, 1, 0, 1, 1, 0, 1] with k = 1, the longest legal window is indexes 0 through 4, the cells [1, 1, 0, 1, 1]. It holds one zero. The length is 5, and the flipped zero stays in the answer.

Longest subarray of 1s after deleting one element uses the same shrink, with at most one zero, and then it changes the length. The stored length is r − l. That is (r − l + 1) − 1. One deletion is required, even when the window holds no zero. An array of n ones returns n − 1.

Walk [1, 1, 0, 1, 1, 0, 1] with that rule. From r = 0 through r = 4 the window starts at 0 and the zero count is 0, then 1. At r = 4 the stored length is 4 − 0, which is 4. That is the five cells minus the one deletion. At r = 5 you add a second zero. The while runs three times: index 0 is a 1, index 1 is a 1, and index 2 is the zero that you drop. l lands on 3 and the zero count is 1 again. The stored length 5 − 3 is 2, which does not beat 4. At r = 6 the length is 3. The answer stays 4. You deleted the zero in [1, 1, 0, 1, 1] and kept four 1s.

On [1, 1, 1] the zero count stays 0, so l stays 0. The stored lengths are 0, then 1, then 2. The answer is 2, which is one less than the length of the array.

r − l is (r − l + 1) − 1. One deletion is required.

function longestSubarray(nums) {
  let l = 0;
  let zeros = 0;
  let best = 0;
  for (let r = 0; r < nums.length; r++) {
    if (nums[r] === 0) zeros += 1;
    while (zeros > 1) {
      if (nums[l] === 0) zeros -= 1;
      l += 1;
    }
    const len = r - l;
    if (len > best) best = len;
  }
  return best;
}

You add to the zero count when nums[r] is 0, and you do that before the while. The while runs while the zero count is greater than 1. It drops nums[l] before l moves. The length you store is r − l, because one deletion is required. If you store r − l + 1 on [1, 1, 1], you return 3, and you have deleted nothing.

A repeat outside the window

Walk the string abba. At r = 0 the letter is a. The map is empty, l stays 0, and you store a at index 0. The length is 1. At r = 1 the letter is b. It is new, l stays 0, and the length is 2.

At r = 2 the letter is b again. The previous b is at index 1, and 1 is still at least l, so that copy is inside the window. You set l to 1 + 1, which is 2. The window is the single b at index 2. The length is 1. You then store the new index of b.

At r = 3 the letter is a. The previous a is at index 0. That index is below l, so the old a is already outside the window. You leave l at 2. The window is indexes 2 and 3, the letters ba, and the length is 2. If you moved l back to 1, the window would be bba, and that repeats b.

Jump l only when the previous index is still inside the window.

function lengthOfLongestSubstring(s) {
  const last = new Map();
  let l = 0;
  let best = 0;
  for (let r = 0; r < s.length; r++) {
    const ch = s[r];
    if (last.has(ch) && last.get(ch) >= l) {
      l = last.get(ch) + 1;
    }
    last.set(ch, r);
    const len = r - l + 1;
    if (len > best) best = len;
  }
  return best;
}
"abba" r=0 a, l=0, len 1 r=1 b, l=0, len 2 r=2 b, previous b is 1, which is >= l, so l = 2, len 1 r=3 a, previous a is 0, which is < l, leave l alone, len 2 best 2 if you moved l back to 1 you would claim "bba", which repeats b

You test the stored index against l before you overwrite it with r. The jump is l = last + 1, and only when that stored index is still at least l. If the stored index is below l, that copy is already outside, and moving l there would pull the left end backward. You write the map after the test, so the map holds the newest index. The length, after the jump, is r − l + 1.

Two kinds of fruit

Fruit into baskets is the same shape with a frequency map. You may hold two distinct values. Walk [1, 2, 1, 2, 3]. Through r = 3 the map holds only 1 and 2, and the window length is 4.

At r = 4 you add 3. The map has three keys, so you shrink. You drop index 0, a 1, and the count of 1 is still above 0. You drop index 1, a 2. You drop index 2, a 1, and the count of 1 hits 0, so you delete that key. l is now 3. The map holds 2 and 3. The length is 2. The best answer stays 4, the band [1, 2, 1, 2].

You add nums[r] first. The while runs while the map has more than two keys. You decrease the count at l, and you delete the key when the count hits 0, before you move l. A key left at count 0 still looks like a third fruit.

Count every valid subarray

Once [l, r] is valid, and every subarray of a valid window is valid, each start from l through r makes a valid subarray that ends at r. There are r − l + 1 of them. You add that number. You do not list them.

Walk [10, 5, 2, 6, 3] with product limit 100. The values are positive, so dividing by nums[l] undoes the multiply. At r = 0 the product is 10. You add 1. At r = 1 the product is 50. You add 2. The count is 3.

At r = 2 the product is 100, which is not below 100. You divide by 10 and move l to 1. The product is 10, so the while stops. You add 2 − 1 + 1, which is 2. The count is 5. At r = 3 the product is 60. You add 3. The count is 8. At r = 4 the product is 180. You divide by 5 and move l to 2. The product is 36. You add 3. The count is 11.

If k is 1 or less, every product of positive integers is at least 1, so the function returns 0 before the loop.

Positive integers. Dividing by nums[l] undoes the multiply.

function numSubarrayProductLessThanK(nums, k) {
  if (k <= 1) return 0;
  let l = 0;
  let prod = 1;
  let count = 0;
  for (let r = 0; r < nums.length; r++) {
    prod *= nums[r];
    while (prod >= k) {
      prod /= nums[l];
      l += 1;
    }
    count += r - l + 1;
  }
  return count;
}

You multiply by nums[r] before the while. The while divides by nums[l] and then moves l, and it keeps going while the product is at least k. You add r − l + 1 after the window is legal. If you add that count before the shrink, you count subarrays whose product is too big.

Exactly K

Exactly K is two at-most counts. atMost(K) counts subarrays with sum at most K, or with at most K distinct values, or with at most K odd numbers. Exactly K is atMost(K) minus atMost(K − 1). This subtraction is legal only when the values are non-negative. Treat an odd number as a 1 when you are counting odds. Binary subarrays with sum, and nice subarrays, are this subtraction.

Walk [1, 0, 1, 0, 1] with goal 2. atMost(2) never has to drop a cell until r = 4, when the sum becomes 3. You drop the leading 1, the sum is 2, and l is 1. That pass adds 14. atMost(1) is stricter. At r = 4 the sum is 2, so the while drops the 0 at index 1, the sum is still 2, and then it drops the 1 at index 2. The sum is 1 and l is 3. That pass adds 10. The difference is 4.

Those four subarrays are [1, 0, 1] at indexes 0 through 2, [1, 0, 1, 0] at indexes 0 through 3, [0, 1, 0, 1] at indexes 1 through 4, and [1, 0, 1] at indexes 2 through 4. Each sums to 2.

Exactly K, for 0/1 arrays. The values are non-negative, so the window is legal.

function numSubarraysWithSum(nums, goal) {
  const atMost = (limit) => {
    if (limit < 0) return 0;
    let l = 0;
    let sum = 0;
    let count = 0;
    for (let r = 0; r < nums.length; r++) {
      sum += nums[r];
      while (sum > limit) {
        sum -= nums[l];
        l += 1;
      }
      count += r - l + 1;
    }
    return count;
  };
  return atMost(goal) - atMost(goal - 1);
}

Inside atMost you add nums[r], then you shrink while the sum is greater than the limit, then you add r − l + 1. The shrink is a while. One drop may leave the sum too high, as the 0 does in atMost(1) at r = 4. If the limit is below 0, the helper returns 0, so atMost(goal − 1) is safe when goal is 0. A negative value breaks the subtraction. On [1, 2, -1, 2] with goal 2, the same helpers return 2. The real count of subarrays with sum 2 is 3. l moved past index 0 when the sum hit 3, and it could not come back for [1, 2, -1].

Cards from the two ends

You take k cards, and each card comes from one of the two ends. That is the same as leaving a middle band of length n − k untouched. The best score is the total minus the minimum sum of a middle window of that length. If k equals n, the middle is empty and the answer is the total.

Take [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 1] and k = 3. The length is 7, so the middle window has length 4. The total is 22. The middle sums are 1+2+3+4 = 10, 2+3+4+5 = 14, 3+4+5+6 = 18, and 4+5+6+1 = 16. The minimum is 10. The score is 22 − 10, which is 12. Those three cards are the right-hand 5, 6, and 1.

You build the total in one pass. You then slide a fixed window of length n − k, subtracting the cell that leaves and adding the cell that enters. You keep the smallest middle sum. You subtract that from the total after the slide finishes. An empty middle, when k equals n, has sum 0, so you must not look for a window of length 0.

Minimum operations to reduce x to zero, when every value is positive, is the same idea from the other side. You remove a prefix and a suffix, and those removed cells together sum to x. A prefix is the stretch from index 0 up to a cut. The leftover middle is the longest subarray whose sum is total − x. The answer is n minus that length, or −1 if that sum never appears. A negative total − x is impossible, and the answer is −1.

Take [1, 1, 4, 2, 3] and x = 5. The total is 11, so the middle must sum to 6. The longest such subarray is [1, 1, 4], length 3. The answer is 5 − 3, which is 2. The removed piece is the suffix [2, 3]. You find that longest middle with the same shrink you used for a target sum, then you subtract its length from n.

A window that is a multiset

Permutation in string and find-all-anagrams share one window whose length is the length of the pattern. You count what the pattern needs. A character becomes matched when its window count equals its needed count. It becomes unmatched when you are about to drop it and the two counts are still equal. When the number of matched characters equals the number of distinct characters in the pattern, the window is an anagram.

Walk s = cbaebabacd and p = abc. The window length is 3. The pattern needs one a, one b, and one c. Indexes 0 through 2 are cba. All three counts match, so you record the start 0. You check that equality before you decrement the leaving c. If you decrement first, cba no longer matches, and you miss index 0.

The window then slides. The next match is indexes 6 through 8, the letters bac. You record 6. Each slide adds the new right character and drops the old left character. You update the matched count when a count hits the needed count, and when a drop makes a matched count fall below the needed count. The answer list is [0, 6].

The shortest covering window

Minimum window substring is the variable window on character counts. missing counts required copies, not distinct letters. You shrink while the window still covers every required copy, and you remember the shortest span.

Walk s = aabbc and t = abc. The need map starts as one a, one b, and one c. missing starts at 3. At r = 0 you take an a. Its need falls to 0, and missing falls to 2. At r = 1 you take another a. Its need falls to −1. That value is below 0, so missing stays 2. The extra a is not a new required copy.

At r = 2 you take a b and missing falls to 1. At r = 3 the extra b drives the need below 0, so missing stays 1. At r = 4 you take the c. missing hits 0. The window aabbc covers the pattern, and its length is 5. You store start 0 and length 5.

The while now drops from the left. You put the first a back. Its need goes from −1 to 0, which is not above 0, so missing stays 0. l moves to 1. The window abbc has length 4, and you store that shorter span. You then put the second a back. Its need climbs from 0 to 1, so missing becomes 1, and the while stops. The answer is abbc. bbc has lost the required a.

Second pass. missing counts required copies, not distinct letters.

function minWindow(s, t) {
  const need = new Map();
  for (const ch of t) need.set(ch, (need.get(ch) ?? 0) + 1);
  let missing = t.length;
  let bestL = 0;
  let bestLen = Infinity;
  let l = 0;
  for (let r = 0; r < s.length; r++) {
    if (need.has(s[r])) {
      need.set(s[r], need.get(s[r]) - 1);
      if (need.get(s[r]) >= 0) missing -= 1;
    }
    while (missing === 0) {
      const len = r - l + 1;
      if (len < bestLen) {
        bestLen = len;
        bestL = l;
      }
      if (need.has(s[l])) {
        need.set(s[l], need.get(s[l]) + 1);
        if (need.get(s[l]) > 0) missing += 1;
      }
      l += 1;
    }
  }
  return bestLen === Infinity ? "" : s.slice(bestL, bestL + bestLen);
}

You decrease the need when s[r] is required, and you decrease missing only when the need is still at least 0 after that decrease. The while runs while missing is 0. You record r − l + 1 before you restore s[l]. You increase missing only when the restored count climbs above 0. Then you move l. If you move l before you record, you save a window that has already lost a required copy.

The maximum inside a sliding window does not fit in one variable. When that maximum slides out of the left edge, the variable does not know the second maximum. Chapter 15 keeps a deque of candidate indexes. On [1, 3, 2, 1] with k = 2, the window slides from [3, 2] to [2, 1]. A variable that only stored 3 still says 3. The maximum is now 2.

Time and extra memory

Each index enters the window once and leaves at most once, so the time is O(n), a sum uses O(1) extra memory, and a frequency map uses extra memory proportional to the distinct values you touch.

Mistakes

  • On [2, 3, 1, 2, 4, 3] with target 7, shrinking with if returns 4. At r = 4 one drop leaves the sum at 7, the if stops, and the later window never reaches [4, 3]. The real answer is 2.
  • On that same input, storing r − l returns 1. The best window is indexes 4 and 5, the cells [4, 3], and r − l is 1. The ordinary length is r − l + 1, which is 2.
  • On [1, 1, 1], longestSubarray must return 2. Storing r − l + 1 returns 3, because that formula never performs the required deletion.
  • On the string abba, moving l to 1 when the old a sits at 0 pulls the left end backward. The window becomes bba, which repeats b. The old a was already outside.
  • On [1, -2, 3] with target 2, the shrink-while-the-sum-is-large loop returns 3. After it drops the leading 1, the sum is 1, so it stops, and it never tests the single cell 3. The real answer is 1. The negative broke the monotone rule.
  • On [1, 2, -1, 2] with goal 2, atMost(2) minus atMost(1) returns 2. Three subarrays actually sum to 2. The window cannot move l back to recover [1, 2, -1].
  • On [1, 3, 2, 1] with window length 2, a single variable still holds 3 after that 3 has left. The window [2, 1] has maximum 2. Chapter 15 is the tool for that.

Practice

Stay before chapter 8. Then before you leave the chapter. The two hard problems are a second pass.

  1. 643. Maximum Average Subarray I easy Stay

    Fixed k. Add the new cell, subtract the cell that fell out.

  2. 209. Minimum Size Subarray Sum medium Stay

    Positive numbers. Shrink while the sum is at least the target. Return 0 if none.

  3. 1004. Max Consecutive Ones III medium Stay

    At most k zeros in the window. The answer is the window length, flips included.

  4. 3. Longest Substring Without Repeating Characters medium Stay

    Jump l to lastIndex + 1 only when lastIndex is still >= l.

  5. 713. Subarray Product Less Than K medium Stay

    Positive integers. Each valid window ending at r contributes r − l + 1.

  6. 1456. Maximum Number of Vowels in a Substring of Given Length medium Then

    Fixed k, but the sum is a vowel count: add 1 or 0, subtract the leaving 1 or 0.

  7. 1493. Longest Subarray of 1's After Deleting One Element medium Then

    At most one zero. Answer is window length minus 1.

  8. 904. Fruit Into Baskets medium Then

    At most two distinct values. Delete a key when its count hits 0.

  9. 930. Binary Subarrays With Sum medium Then

    Exactly goal, on 0/1 values: atMost(goal) − atMost(goal − 1).

  10. 1248. Count Number of Nice Subarrays medium Then

    Exactly k odd numbers. Treat odd as 1 and reuse the subtraction.

  11. 567. Permutation in String medium Then

    Fixed window of length s1. Compare counts, or track how many characters are matched.

  12. 438. Find All Anagrams in a String medium Then

    The same window. Record the start index every time the window matches.

  13. 1423. Maximum Points You Can Obtain from Cards medium Then

    Total minus the minimum middle window of length n − k.

  14. 1658. Minimum Operations to Reduce X to Zero medium Then

    Positive nums. Longest subarray with sum total − x, then n minus that length.

  15. 76. Minimum Window Substring hard Second pass

    Shrink while every required character is covered. Keep the shortest span.

  16. 992. Subarrays with K Different Integers hard Second pass

    atMost(K) − atMost(K − 1) with a frequency map of values.

Rewrite from memory

Rewrite the minimum-length window and the exactly-K subtraction. Say the length out loud as r minus l plus 1.

Carry forward. Chapter 8 answers the sum questions a window must refuse: negatives, and counting every subarray whose sum is K.

Chapter 08 · builds on chapters 2 and 7

Prefix sums

A prefix stores the sum of a starting piece. You ask how many earlier prefixes equal sum minus k, and only then do you record the sum you have now.

What this pattern is

A prefix is the sum of a starting piece of the array. prefix[0] is 0. That 0 is the empty prefix, the sum before you have read any cell. prefix[i] is the sum of the first i cells, which is nums[0] + … + nums[i − 1].

Picture a taxi meter. The cost of the ride from street i through street j, both ends included, is the meter just after j minus the meter at i. In the array, that sum is prefix[j + 1] − prefix[i].

A remainder is what is left after you divide a prefix by k. A subarray is a stretch of cells with no gaps. Two prefixes with the same remainder differ by a multiple of k, so the subarray between them is divisible by k.

You keep the prefixes you have already finished in a map. At the current sum you ask how many earlier prefixes equal sum − k. You record the current sum only after that question.

What you already know

Chapter 2 kept one running total while you walked. Chapter 7 used a running total inside a window, the band from index l through index r, and it had to refuse the job when a negative could make the sum fall. A prefix keeps every running total, not only the total inside the current band. From those totals you can read any subarray sum, and you can count the subarrays that add up to k.

A worked example

Use [2, 1, -1, 3, 2] and k = 3. The map starts with the empty prefix: key 0 has count 1. The sum starts at 0. You have not stored any cell yet.

At index 0 you add 2. The sum is 2. You ask for 2 − 3, which is −1. The map has no −1, so the count stays 0. You then record 2. The map is {0: 1, 2: 1}.

At index 1 you add 1. The sum is 3. You ask for 0. The seed answers once. That hit is the subarray from the start, the cells [2, 1]. The count is 1. You then record 3.

At index 2 you add −1. The sum is 2. You ask for −1 and miss. You then record 2. The key 2 now has count 2, because the sum was 2 after index 0 and it is 2 again after index 2. You record that second 2 only after the question, so later indexes can see both copies and this index cannot see the copy it just made.

At index 3 you add 3. The sum is 5. You ask for 2. The map answers 2. One earlier prefix is the sum after index 0, so the subarray is [1, -1, 3]. The other is the sum after index 2, so the subarray is [3]. The count is 3. You then record 5.

At index 4 you add 2. The sum is 7. You ask for 4 and miss. The count stays 3. Those three subarrays are the only ones that add to 3. If k were 0, recording the sum before the question would count an empty piece, because sum − k would be the sum you just stored.

The same query-then-record order on a smaller array is this trace.

[1, 2, 3], k = 3 start {0: 1} sum 1, ask for -2, miss, record 1 sum 3, ask for 0, hit 1, record 3 subarray [1, 2] sum 6, ask for 3, hit 1, record 6 subarray [3] count = 2 if you incremented first, a target of 0 would count the empty prefix as a new subarray

How you recognise it

  • You need the sum of many ranges, and one pass can build the prefix before you answer them.
  • You need the number of subarrays whose sum is k, and the values may be negative or zero.
  • You need the longest subarray with sum k, and a negative means you keep the first index of each prefix.
  • The test is about a remainder, so two prefixes match when their repaired remainders are equal.
  • A window is the wrong tool because adding a cell can make the running sum smaller.

Some prompts look close and belong in another chapter.

  • Use chapter 7 when every value is non-negative and you want the shortest subarray whose sum is at least k.
  • Use chapter 11 when you want the one subarray with the largest sum, and an empty subarray is not allowed.
  • Use chapter 4 when you need two cells that add to a target, and those cells do not have to form a subarray.

What to say out loud

An invariant is a fact you keep true on every step. The map holds prefixes you have already finished, and the empty prefix is stored as 0 with count 1.

You add the next cell into the sum. You ask for sum − k. Only then do you add one to the count of the sum you are standing on.

The template

Seed 0 → 1. Query, then increment.

function buildPrefix(nums) {
  const prefix = [0];
  for (const x of nums) prefix.push(prefix[prefix.length - 1] + x);
  return prefix;
}

function subarraySum(nums, k) {
  const freq = new Map();
  freq.set(0, 1);
  let sum = 0;
  let count = 0;
  for (const x of nums) {
    sum += x;
    count += freq.get(sum - k) ?? 0;
    freq.set(sum, (freq.get(sum) ?? 0) + 1);
  }
  return count;
}

Build the prefix by starting with [0]. For each value, push the last prefix plus that value. prefix[0] stays 0, the empty prefix. On [2, 1, -1, 3, 2] the prefix is [0, 2, 3, 2, 5, 7]. The inclusive sum of indexes 1 through 3 is the cells 1, −1, and 3. That is prefix[4] − prefix[1], because j is 3 and i is 1, so you read prefix[j + 1] − prefix[i]. The difference is 5 − 2, which is 3.

In the counting loop the seed is freq.set(0, 1) before you read a cell. Without that seed, a subarray that starts at index 0 is invisible. On [1, 2] with k = 3, the sum becomes 3 and the question is 0. If 0 is missing, the count stays 0, and you miss [1, 2].

The order inside the loop is fixed. Add the cell, ask for sum − k, then increment. If you increment first, a target of 0 counts an empty piece. On [1] with k = 0, no non-empty subarray sums to 0. The wrong order stores 1 and then asks for 1, so it returns 1. That 1 is the empty piece from the prefix to itself.

The longest subarray with sum k stores the first index of each prefix, seeded as 0 at index −1. When you see sum − k, the length is the current index minus that first index. You do not overwrite an earlier index, because an earlier start makes a longer subarray. On [1, -1, 1, -1] with k = 0, the prefixes are 1, 0, 1, 0. The first 0 is the seed at −1, so the length at the end is 4. If you store the later 0 instead, the length you report is 2.

The same remainder

JavaScript keeps the sign of the dividend. (-7) % 5 is −2, not 3. Two prefixes are the same remainder only after you repair the value. The repair is ((sum % k) + k) % k. For −7 and k = 5, that expression is 3. The bare −2 and the repaired 3 differ by a multiple of 5, and they miss each other if you store the raw result.

Walk [2, -3, 1, 3, 2] with k = 5. The map again starts as {0: 1}. Index 0 makes the sum 2. The repaired remainder is 2. You miss, then you store 2.

Index 1 makes the sum −1. In JavaScript (-1) % 5 is −1. The repair turns that into 4. You store 4, not −1. Index 2 makes the sum 0. The remainder is 0, and the seed answers once. The subarray [2, -3, 1] sums to 0, which is divisible by 5. The count is 1. You then increment the count of 0, so the key 0 now holds 2.

Index 3 makes the sum 3. The remainder is 3. You miss. Index 4 makes the sum 5. The remainder is 0. The map answers 2. One hit is the whole array, which sums to 5. The other is [3, 2], which also sums to 5. The count is 3. You asked for the repaired remainder before you incremented it, the same order as the sum map.

The added k folds a negative remainder back into 0..k−1.

function subarraysDivByK(nums, k) {
  const freq = new Map();
  freq.set(0, 1);
  let sum = 0;
  let count = 0;
  for (const x of nums) {
    sum += x;
    const mod = ((sum % k) + k) % k;
    count += freq.get(mod) ?? 0;
    freq.set(mod, (freq.get(mod) ?? 0) + 1);
  }
  return count;
}
(-7) % 5 in JavaScript is -2 ((-7 % 5) + 5) % 5 = 3 that matches 3, because -7 and 3 differ by -10, a multiple of 5

You add the cell, you repair the remainder, you query that repaired value, and then you increment it. The added k folds a negative remainder back into the range 0 through k − 1. If you store the raw %, the −2 bucket and the 3 bucket never meet. The seed 0 with count 1 is still required, so a prefix that is itself divisible by k is counted.

Length at least 2

Continuous subarray sum asks whether some subarray of length at least 2 has a sum divisible by k. Current constraints have k at least 1, so you will not divide by zero. You seed remainder 0 at index −1. You store only the first index of each remainder. A later copy at index i is a hit when i − first >= 2.

Walk [23, 2, 4, 6, 7] with k = 6. At index 0 the sum is 23 and the remainder is 5. The map does not have 5, so you store 5 at index 0. At index 1 the sum is 25 and the remainder is 1. You store 1 at index 1.

At index 2 the sum is 29 and the remainder is 5. The map already has 5, stored at index 0. 2 − 0 is 2, which is at least 2, so you return true. The subarray is indexes 1 through 2, the cells [2, 4], and the sum is 6. You do not replace the stored 0 with 2. The first index is the one that makes the span long enough.

Do not update an index you have already stored.

function checkSubarraySum(nums, k) {
  const first = new Map();
  first.set(0, -1);
  let sum = 0;
  for (let i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
    sum += nums[i];
    const mod = ((sum % k) + k) % k;
    if (first.has(mod)) {
      if (i - first.get(mod) >= 2) return true;
    } else {
      first.set(mod, i);
    }
  }
  return false;
}
[23, 2, 4, 6, 7], k = 6 i=0 sum 23, mod 5, store 5 at 0 i=1 sum 25, mod 1, store 1 at 1 i=2 sum 29, mod 5, 2 − 0 = 2, return true the subarray [2, 4] sums to 6

You repair the remainder before you look in the map. If the key is already there, you test the distance and you do not write. You write the index only when the remainder is new. The seed puts 0 at −1 so a subarray that begins at index 0 can be long enough. On [5, 5] with k = 5, the second remainder 0 is two steps from −1, and [5, 5] is a hit. If you overwrite the first index, a long enough span shrinks to a short one. On [5, 0, 0] with k = 5, replacing −1 with 0 makes every later step look one cell too short, and the function returns false. [5, 0] and [0, 0] are both valid.

The same count of zeros and ones

An equal number of 0s and 1s becomes a prefix sum if you map 0 to −1 and 1 to +1. A subarray with sum 0 is a balance. You seed index −1 at prefix 0, and you store the first index only.

Walk [0, 1, 0, 1] after the map, so the values are [-1, 1, -1, 1]. The running sums are −1, 0, −1, and 0. At index 1 the sum is 0. The seed sits at −1, so the length is 2. That subarray is [0, 1]. You do not overwrite the seed. At index 3 the sum is 0 again. The length from −1 is 4, the whole array, which has two 0s and two 1s. If you had stored the later index of 0, this last step would report length 2 and you would miss the longer balance.

You write an index only the first time you see that prefix. Each later copy uses the stored index to measure the length. The answer is the largest of those lengths.

Time and extra memory

You walk the array once and each map operation is expected constant time, so the time is O(n) and the map uses O(n) extra memory; a prefix array for range queries is O(n) to build and then O(1) per query.

Mistakes

  • On [1, 2] with k = 3, forgetting the seed 0 → 1 returns 0. The subarray [1, 2] starts at index 0, and it needs the empty prefix.
  • On [1] with k = 0, incrementing the frequency before the query returns 1. No non-empty subarray sums to 0. The extra 1 is the empty piece.
  • On a running sum of −7 with k = 5, the raw JavaScript remainder is −2. The repaired remainder is 3. Storing −2 splits the buckets, so a later prefix with remainder 3 misses it.
  • On [1, -1, 1, -1] with k = 0, storing the latest index of prefix 0 returns 2. The longest subarray with sum 0 is the whole array, length 4.
  • On [5, 5] with k = 5, a continuous-subarray check with no seed at −1 returns false. [5, 5] sums to 10, and the distance from the missing seed is what makes the length 2.
  • On [5, 0, 0] with k = 5, overwriting the first index of remainder 0 returns false. The spans [5, 0] and [0, 0] both qualify, and each later write makes the distance look shorter than 2.

Practice

Stay before chapter 9. Then before you leave the chapter.

  1. 303. Range Sum Query - Immutable easy Stay

    Build prefix once in the constructor. sumRange(i, j) is prefix[j + 1] − prefix[i].

  2. 560. Subarray Sum Equals K medium Stay

    Seed 0 → 1. Add the count of sum − k, then increment sum.

  3. 525. Contiguous Array medium Stay

    Map 0 to −1. Longest prefix balance of 0. Seed index −1, store the first index.

  4. 974. Subarray Sums Divisible by K medium Then

    Frequency of the repaired remainder. The repair is ((sum % k) + k) % k.

  5. 523. Continuous Subarray Sum medium Then

    First index only, seed −1, length at least 2. k ≥ 1 under the current constraints.

Rewrite from memory

Rewrite the frequency map, including the seed 0 to 1 and the query before the update, and the repair for a negative remainder.

Carry forward. Chapter 9 runs this idea backward. Instead of summing a range, you add a value onto a range with two writes.

Chapter 09 · builds on chapter 8

Difference array

A range add is two marks. One later scan turns those marks back into the array.

What this pattern is

A difference array queues range additions. You want to add a value onto many ranges, and you will read the array only after every update is queued. Each range becomes two writes. One later scan rebuilds the array.

Picture a row of fence planks. To paint planks L through R with 10 more coats, you mark +10 on plank L and −10 on the plank just after R. You do not touch the planks in between. One walk along the fence applies the marks: the running total goes up when a mark starts, and it goes down when a mark stops.

For an inclusive range [L, R], the stop mark sits at R + 1. Index R still receives the value. The difference array has one extra cell so a mark that lands just past the end has a place to sit.

What you already know

Chapter 8 builds a prefix, the sum of a starting piece, so you can read a range sum in constant time after a linear build. A difference array is that idea run backward. You already know a scan can turn a list of changes into the values you stand on. Here you queue the changes first, two writes per range, and you scan once at the end.

A worked example

Take n = 5, so the real indexes are 0 through 4, and give the difference array a sixth cell at index 5. It starts as six zeros. You will add 10 on the inclusive range [1, 3], then add 5 on the inclusive range [2, 4].

The first update writes +10 at index 1 and −10 at index 4, because R + 1 is 4. The array is [0, 10, 0, 0, -10, 0]. Index 3 has no mark of its own. It will still receive the 10, because the +10 is still in the running total when you arrive there.

The second update writes +5 at index 2 and −5 at index 5. The −5 lands in the extra cell, past the last real index. The array is [0, 10, 5, 0, -10, -5].

Now you scan. The running value starts at 0. At index 0 you add 0, so the cell is 0. Nothing was added at the start. At index 1 you add 10, so the cell is 10. The first range has turned on.

At index 2 you add 5, so the cell is 15. Both ranges cover index 2. At index 3 you add 0, so the cell stays 15. Index 3 is inside [1, 3] and inside [2, 4]. The running value carries the earlier marks. You did not need a mark on the plank itself.

At index 4 you add −10, so the cell is 5. The first range ended at 3, so index 4 must lose the 10. The second range still covers 4, so 5 remains. You never copy cell 5 into the answer. It holds the −5 that would cancel the second range if the array continued. The final array is [0, 10, 15, 15, 5].

If the first update had subtracted at R instead of R + 1, the −10 would land on index 3. Index 3 would become 5, and it would miss the 10 that the inclusive range promised it.

The marks and the scan, in a compact trace, look like this.

n = 5, add 10 on [1, 3], add 5 on [2, 4] diff starts [0, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0] after [1, 3]: [0, 10, 0, 0, -10, 0] after [2, 4]: [0, 10, 5, 0, -10, -5] scan: 0, 10, 15, 15, 5

How you recognise it

  • You add a value onto many ranges, and you read the array only after every update is queued.
  • Each event has a start and an end, such as a booking, a ride, or a birth and a death.
  • The timeline is a line of slots, such as flight numbers, stops on a road, or years from 1950 to 2050.
  • Each update can be a mark where the change starts and a mark where the change stops.
  • You do not need the array in the middle of the updates. One scan at the end is enough.

Some prompts look close and belong in another chapter.

  • Use chapter 8 when you will read many range sums, and you are not adding a value onto many ranges.
  • Use chapter 7 when one fixed-length band slides forward and you only add the new cell and drop the old cell.
  • Use chapter 21 when the ranges are rectangles on a grid and you need a two-dimensional prefix.

What to say out loud

An invariant is a fact you keep true after every mark. diff[i] is the change in the running value as you step onto index i.

A range from L through R, both ends included, writes +v at L and −v at R + 1. The scan adds those marks into a running value and writes that value into the answer.

The template

The extra cell holds the −v that would have landed just past the end.

function applyRanges(n, updates) {
  const diff = new Array(n + 1).fill(0);
  for (const [L, R, v] of updates) {
    diff[L] += v;
    diff[R + 1] -= v;
  }
  const arr = new Array(n).fill(0);
  let run = 0;
  for (let i = 0; i < n; i++) {
    run += diff[i];
    arr[i] = run;
  }
  return arr;
}

You apply every update as two writes before you read any answer cell. Inclusive [L, R] is +v at L and −v at R + 1. diff has length n + 1 so the minus can land just past the end.

The scan adds diff[i] into the running value and then writes the answer cell. You do not read diff[n] into the answer. If you subtract at R, index R loses the value on the same step it should receive it. On the first update above, that mistake turns index 3 from 15 into 5 once the second update is included, because the 10 is cancelled one plank too soon.

Flights numbered from 1

Corporate flight bookings are 1-based. Flight numbers run from 1 to n. A booking of seats on flights first through last, both ends included, is not a write at index first. You write diff[first − 1] += seats and diff[last] −= seats.

Take n = 5 and one booking of 10 seats on flights 2 through 4. Flight 2 is index 1, so you add 10 at index 1. The subtraction lands at index 4, which is flight 5, the first flight that should not receive the seats. The scan produces [0, 10, 10, 10, 0]. Flights 2, 3, and 4 gained the seats.

When last equals n, the subtraction hits the extra cell and never enters the answer. A booking of 10 seats on flight 1 only, with n = 1, writes +10 at index 0 and −10 at index 1. The scan returns [10]. If you treat the flight number as an index and write both marks on index 1, they cancel, and flight 1 stays 0.

Passengers leave at the destination

Car pooling uses a half-open ride. Passengers board at from and leave at to, so they do not occupy the destination. You write diff[from] += passengers and diff[to] −= passengers. You scan the road. If the running load ever exceeds capacity, you return false. The timeline only needs to be as long as the farthest stop.

Take capacity 2, a trip of 2 passengers from stop 1 to stop 3, and a trip of 2 passengers from stop 3 to stop 4. The first trip adds 2 at stop 1 and subtracts 2 at stop 3. The second trip adds 2 at stop 3 and subtracts 2 at stop 4. At stop 1 the load is 2. At stop 2 the load is 2. At stop 3 the first group leaves and the second group boards, so the load stays 2. The load never exceeds 2, so the answer is true.

You add the board mark and the leave mark for every trip before you scan. During the scan you add diff[i] and then you compare the load with capacity. If you keep the first group through the destination, stop 3 holds both groups and the load is 4. That wrong code returns false on this input. The passengers from the first trip are already gone at stop 3.

A tie keeps the earlier year

Maximum population year is the same two writes on a tiny timeline. A person is alive from birth inclusive to death exclusive. You shift the years by 1950 so the indexes start at 0. When two years tie, you keep the earlier one. You update the best year only when the running population is strictly greater.

Take one person born in 1950 who dies in 1960. That person is alive in 1950 through 1959. You add 1 at index 1950 − 1950, which is 0, and you subtract 1 at index 1960 − 1950, which is 10. The scan sees population 1 for ten years, then 0.

The first year with population 1 is 1950. Every later year through 1959 ties that population. A strict greater test leaves the stored year at 1950. If you update whenever the population is greater than or equal to the best, each tied year replaces the stored year, and you return 1959. Death is exclusive, so 1960 itself is not a living year and must not receive the +1.

Coverage and a dot product

Maximum sum of any permutation scores each index by how many requests cover it. The difference array builds that coverage. You sort the values and you sort the coverages. You pair the largest value with the most requested index. You take the dot product modulo 1e9+7. You reduce the sum modulo at every step so the product stays inside a safe JavaScript integer.

Take three indexes and two requests, [0, 1] and [1, 2]. The first request writes +1 at 0 and −1 at 2. The second writes +1 at 1 and −1 at 3. The scan gives coverage [1, 2, 1]. Index 1 is covered twice, and the ends are covered once.

If the values are [1, 2, 3], you sort them to [3, 2, 1] and you sort the coverage to [2, 1, 1]. The dot product is 3 * 2 + 2 * 1 + 1 * 1, which is 9. You place the biggest value on the busiest index, then the next biggest on the next busiest index. The order of those two sorts is the whole second step.

Flips as a running parity

Minimum number of K consecutive bit flips stores each flip as parity. A window here is the band of k neighboring cells that one flip covers. The running value is the number of active flips modulo 2. If the current bit, after the flips already started, is 0, you must start a flip at i. That flip adds 1 now and expires k steps later. If that end would fall past n, you return −1.

Walk [0, 1, 0] with k = 1. At index 0 the running parity is 0, and (0 + 0) is even, so the bit you see is 0. A flip of length 1 ends at index 1, which is inside the array. You count one flip, the parity becomes 1, and you write −1 at diff[1].

At index 1 you add that −1, so the parity returns to 0. The stored bit is 1, and (1 + 0) is odd, so you do not flip. At index 2 the parity is 0 and the stored bit is 0, so you see a 0. A flip of length 1 ends at index 3, which is allowed. You count a second flip. The answer is 2. The first flip turns the array into [1, 1, 0], and the second turns it into [1, 1, 1].

The same array with k = 3 fails. Index 0 starts a flip, and the parity becomes 1. At index 1 the bit you see is (1 + 1) modulo 2, which is 0, so you need another flip. i + k is 4, which is past n. You return −1. The only flip that fits, the one that starts at 0, turns [0, 1, 0] into [1, 0, 1] and leaves index 1 at 0.

Second pass. Starting a flip is a +1 that expires at i + k.

function minKBitFlips(nums, k) {
  const n = nums.length;
  const diff = new Array(n + 1).fill(0);
  let cur = 0;
  let flips = 0;
  for (let i = 0; i < n; i++) {
    cur += diff[i];
    if ((nums[i] + cur) % 2 === 0) {
      if (i + k > n) return -1;
      flips += 1;
      cur += 1;
      diff[i + k] -= 1;
    }
  }
  return flips;
}

You add diff[i] into the running parity before you look at nums[i]. The bit you see is (nums[i] + cur) % 2. When that is 0, you need a flip at i. If i + k is greater than n, you return −1. Otherwise you add 1 to the flip count, add 1 to the running parity, and you write −1 at diff[i + k] so the flip expires when the window ends. You do not also write +1 at diff[i], because you already added that 1 into the running parity for the current index.

Time and extra memory

Each update is two writes and the final scan visits each slot once, so the time is O(n + u) for u updates and the extra memory is O(n).

Mistakes

  • On n = 5 with +10 on inclusive [1, 3], subtracting at 3 instead of 4 drops index 3. That index is inside the range, and the wrong scan never leaves the 10 there.
  • On n = 1 with 10 seats booked on flight 1, treating the flight number as an index writes +10 and −10 on the same cell. The answer comes back [0]. The shifted write, +10 at index 0 and −10 at index 1, returns [10].
  • On capacity 2 with passengers 2 from stop 1 to stop 3 and passengers 2 from stop 3 to stop 4, occupying the destination returns false. The load at stop 3 looks like 4. The first group has already left, and the real load is 2.
  • On a person alive from 1950 inclusive to 1960 exclusive, updating the best year on a tie returns 1959. The population is 1 for every year from 1950 through 1959, and the earlier year 1950 is the one you keep.

Practice

Stay before chapter 10. The last two are a second pass.

  1. 1854. Maximum Population Year easy Stay

    Difference on years 1950..2050. Death is exclusive. Ties keep the earlier year.

  2. 1109. Corporate Flight Bookings medium Stay

    1-based. diff[first − 1] += seats and diff[last] −= seats.

  3. 1094. Car Pooling medium Stay

    Half-open trip [from, to). Fail as soon as the running load exceeds capacity.

  4. 1589. Maximum Sum Obtained of Any Permutation hard Second pass

    Coverage by a difference array, sort both sides, dot product, modulo 1e9+7.

  5. 995. Minimum Number of K Consecutive Bit Flips hard Second pass

    A flip is a parity update of length k. Return −1 when a needed flip runs off the end.

Rewrite from memory

Rewrite the inclusive update, plus v at L and minus v at R plus 1, and the scan that rebuilds the array.

Carry forward. Chapter 10 builds a full left summary and a full right summary, then reads them together at each index.

Chapter 10 · builds on chapters 2 and 5

A left pass and a right pass

You build one summary from the left and one from the right. At each index you read the two summaries together.

What this pattern is

Some answers need both directions. You build one array from left to right. You build another from right to left. Then you combine them at each index.

Picture yourself standing at one bar in a row of bars. You look left and remember the tallest bar on that side. You look right and remember the tallest bar on that side. The water at your feet is the lower of those two heights, minus the bar you are standing on. A product of every cell except this one is the same shape: the product of everything to the left, times the product of everything to the right.

The extra memory is often O(n) for the two summaries. The product routine can store the left products in the answer array, so the extra memory beyond the answer is constant. The passes are still linear.

What you already know

Chapter 2 was enough when the answer at i used only the cells you had already seen, or only a scan that ran backward. Chapter 5 moved inward from the two ends when each step could drop one end. Here you finish a summary of the whole left side and a summary of the whole right side, and you read the pair at i.

A worked example

Use [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]. You want the product of every cell except the one you are on. You will not use division. left starts at 1, which is the product of no cells.

At index 0 you write left into the answer before you multiply. The answer cell becomes 1. Then left becomes 1 * 1, which is 1. At index 1 you write 1, then left becomes 1 * 2, which is 2. At index 2 you write 2, then left becomes 6. At index 3 you write 6, then left becomes 24. At index 4 you write 24, then left becomes 120. The answer so far is [1, 1, 2, 6, 24]. Each cell holds the product of the cells strictly before it.

You now walk backward. right starts at 1, the product of no cells to the right of the end. At index 4 you multiply the answer cell by right before you fold nums[4] into right. 24 * 1 is 24, and right becomes 5.

At index 3 the answer cell is 6, and right is 5, so the cell becomes 30. Then right becomes 5 * 4, which is 20. At index 2 the cell 2 becomes 2 * 20, which is 40, and right becomes 60. At index 1 the cell 1 becomes 60, and right becomes 120. At index 0 the cell 1 becomes 120, and right becomes 120.

The answer is [120, 60, 40, 30, 24]. Check index 2: the product of everything except 3 is 1 * 2 * 4 * 5, which is 40. The left product written at that index was 2, and the right product multiplied in was 20. The cell's own value, 3, was never written into its own answer.

The order is the whole trick. You write the left product before you multiply nums[i] into left. If you multiply first on this array, the left pass writes [1, 2, 6, 24, 120], and every cell already contains itself.

How you recognise it

  • The value at index i depends on a summary of every cell to its left and a summary of every cell to its right.
  • You need a product of the other cells, the water above a bar, the length of a mountain, or a candy count forced by both neighbors.
